DARPA Lift Challenge ends with a surprisingly old-fashioned winner
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:45:01 UTC
00:45:01 KST

- DARPA's Lift Challenge concluded after seven days, with Team Avidrone winning first place at a 3.84 to 1 payload-to-weight ratio.
- Avidrone's Katana helicopter drone carried 112.4 pounds (51 kilograms) while weighing 29.3 pounds (13.3 kilograms), earning $1.25 million.
A team of engineers have proved that a helicopter design older than most of their grandparents can still out-fly the strangest, most futuristic drone shapes engineers could dream up. The Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency, the Pentagon’s advanced research arm known for funding technologies that eventually reshape entire industries, announced that its Lift Challenge had wrapped after seven days of competition, crowning Team Avidrone the winner with a payload-to-weight ratio of 3.84 to 1.
DARPA launched the Lift Challenge last November with a prize pool of $6.5 million and a single, brutally simple goal: build a drone that can carry a payload weighing more than four times its own body weight, a threshold no existing heavy-lift drone comes close to reaching. Conventional delivery and cargo drones on the market today typically top out at payload ratios well under 2 to 1, meaning a drone that weighs 50 pounds (23 kilograms) might struggle to carry even 100 pounds (45 kilograms) of cargo. DARPA’s bet was that opening the challenge to unconventional designs, rather than dictating a specific engineering approach, would push someone past the 4 to 1 mark the same way earlier DARPA competitions produced breakthroughs in autonomous vehicles and computer networking.
The competition unfolded at the National Museum of the U.S. Air Force in Dayton, Ohio, where DARPA had invited 124 teams to compete and 76 actually made it to the flight line, according to DARPA’s own accounting of the event. Teams flew a five-nautical-mile (9.3 kilometer) circuit course in head-to-head live trials, taking off with a loaded payload, flying a set distance, dropping the cargo, and completing the remaining distance empty, all within a 30-minute time limit. The format rewarded not just raw lifting power but reliability, since a single failed run eliminated a team’s chance at that day’s ranking.
Avidrone Inc. entered a design that stood out precisely because it wasn’t exotic. While rival teams flew multirotor drones and experimental configurations built specifically to chase the payload-to-weight metric, Avidrone’s aircraft, named Katana, used a conventional single-main-rotor helicopter layout with a tail boom, the same basic configuration that has defined production helicopters since the 1940s, according to DroneXL‘s reporting on the event. The Katana weighed 29.3 pounds (13.3 kilograms) and carried a payload of 112.4 pounds (51 kilograms) of cast iron around the course, producing the 3.84 to 1 ratio DARPA confirmed in its announcement. DroneXL and DefPost both reported that Avidrone completed its winning run in 19 minutes and 17 seconds, well inside the 30-minute limit, and DefPost added that the Katana’s aircraft platform, propulsion system, and flight-control technologies were all developed in-house by the company. A component list published by DefPost pointed to battery-electric propulsion, with battery modules supplied by Titan Batteries powering motors run through separate speed controllers.
No team reached the 4 to 1 target DARPA set when it opened the competition, a fact that triggered a prize rule DroneXL had flagged before the event’s first weigh-in: because the top ratio fell short of the stated goal, every podium payout was cut in half. Avidrone collected $1.25 million instead of the $2.5 million top prize, MTech Operations LLC took second place with a 3.64 to 1 ratio for $750,000, and Xtreme Aerial Concepts finished third at 3.45 to 1 for $500,000, according to DroneXL’s reporting.
The runner-up and third-place finishers took very different paths to their results. Coverage from ENGtechnica noted that other competitors explored configurations far removed from anything flying commercially today, including a design from RotoWings built around two radio-controlled airplanes connected together to form one large rotating system, and a tandem-rotor aircraft from Phoenix Drone LLC built to resemble a scaled-down Chinook.
That range of approaches, from Avidrone’s deliberately conventional helicopter to designs that looked nothing like any aircraft currently in service, captures what DARPA challenges are built to surface: not a single predetermined answer, but a wide field of competing ideas tested against the same unforgiving standard.
Aviation Week, which covered the competition’s final days, framed the results as falling short of DARPA’s stated goal while still meaningfully resetting expectations for what small drones can carry, noting that the event ran from August 4 through 9 at the Dayton museum.
What DARPA proved this month goes beyond a single number on a scoreboard, showing that the agency’s decades-old formula, hand a hard problem to dozens of competing teams and let the best design win, still works even when the winning idea looks less like science fiction and more like something that has been flying since before jet engines existed.

North Korean ballistic missile kills 7 steelworkers on their way to shelter, shuts down Zaporizhstal plant
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:44:16 UTC
00:44:16 KST

A Russian ballistic strike on Zaporizhzhia, Ukraine, killed seven employees of the Zaporizhstal steel plant and injured 21 others, Ukrainian steelmaker Metinvest, the plant’s majority owner, said on 11 August.
The workers were on their way to a shelter after an air raid alert was announced when the strike hit the industrial site, according to the company.
Metinvest said the attack also damaged energy facilities and infrastructure, as well as the main and auxiliary facilities of the plant’s coke and blast-furnace operations.
Russia’s ballistic missile strikes leave very little time between threat detection and impact for people to react and reach shelter. Several people have been killed from strikes across the country while on their way to bomb shelters.
Zaporizhstal shuts down after strike
Metinvest said Zaporizhstal has been completely shut down following the attack, while its other production sites in the city are operating at reduced capacity.
Company specialists are assessing the damage and determining the scope and timeline of repairs. Emergency and restoration work is already underway, according to the statement.
The company said its Zaporizhzhia facilities are civilian industrial sites that employ tens of thousands of people and produce steelmaking and coke-chemical products.
Metinvest said it is providing medical assistance and support to the 21 injured employees and staying in contact with the families of those killed and wounded.
The company's facilities will fly flags at half-mast on 11 and 12 August in mourning for the seven Zaporizhstal workers killed in the attack.
North Korean missiles used against Zaporizhzhia
Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said Zaporizhzhia was targeted overnight with North Korean ballistic missiles, Russian Zircon missiles and guided aerial bombs.
Zelenskyy said the attack was designed to cause maximum damage to civilian infrastructure.
Ukraine's military intelligence said on 5 August that a North Korean missile unit had begun deploying to western Russia, potentially adding up to 120 ballistic missiles and six launchers to Russia's arsenal.
According to Ukrainian intelligence representative Andrii Cherniak, North Korea has supplied Russia with around 150 KN-23 and KN-24 missiles since late 2023, including a recent batch of 40 missiles.
The missiles have been linked to attacks with high civilian casualties, including a July strike on Radushne in Dnipropetrovsk Oblast that killed an entire family.
Ballistic threat is growing
The strike comes as Russia has increasingly relied on ballistic missiles in attacks on Ukrainian cities. Unlike drones and cruise missiles, ballistic missiles travel at very high speeds and are much harder for Ukraine to intercept, particularly because Kyiv has a limited supply of the advanced air defense interceptors needed to counter them, mainly US-made Patriot systems.
Ukraine has reported that most ballistic missiles launched in recent mass attacks have penetrated its air defenses, contributing to severe destruction and civilian casualties.

Colombia earthquake strikes at challenging time for aid response
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:40:49 UTC
00:40:49 KST

Share & more
Aid organisations responding to the powerful earthquake that hit Colombia on 10 August face significant challenges, including difficulties accessing some of the worst-hit areas in remote western regions that have been seeing rising violence.
Emergency response teams are still assessing the damage, but at least 181 people have already been reported killed and nearly 1,250 injured. It is feared the toll could rise significantly, with many more trapped under the rubble or missing.
Speaking at a press conference hours after the 7.4-magnitude quake struck at 7:34am on Monday, President Abelardo de la Espriella, who only took office last week, said more than 1,500 residential buildings, 18 health centres, and 52 schools had been damaged. An update on 11 August from the Colombian Association of Capital Cities (Asocapitales) said 153 buildings had collapsed.
The earthquake comes at a tough time for Colombia. The country was already facing its worst humanitarian crisis in years, with rising violence by armed groups making it increasingly difficult for aid groups to address mounting needs in conflict zones.
“Local communities were already facing armed conflict and high levels of poverty long before the ground began to shake,” Nicole Kast, the International Rescue Committee’s country director for Colombia, Venezuela, and Ecuador, said in a press release issued on the day of the quake. “We are deeply concerned that today's natural disaster will make the needs on the ground even steeper as people lose access to health care and other essential services."
In Chocó department, where the earthquake’s epicentre is located, restrictions on humanitarian aid are common. The region is not only one of the poorest in the country but also one of the hardest hit by armed conflict, with more than 21,000 people experiencing forced confinement – the highest number in the country, according to Colombia’s Ombudsman’s Office.
Armed groups have sought to increase their control over communities as they fight each other for control over key territories, leaving remote communities even more isolated. The quake has now rendered some of them – including Chocó’s San Juan and Baudó river regions – completely unreachable.
“Chocó subregions such as San Juan and Baudó are currently inaccessible and isolated,” Shirley Hidalgo, senior director of operations of World Vision in Colombia, told The New Humanitarian. “The local authorities are asking for urgent medical supplies and support to address the needs of this department.”
But it may be hard for aid to arrive soon. And not only in Chocó. The situation in that department is mirrored in other places affected by rising violence.
According to the UN’s emergency aid coordination body, OCHA, more than 1.6 million people in the country were affected by violence and armed conflict last year – three times the figure in 2024. Some 96,000 people were forcibly displaced and 155,000 were confined by curfews, armed strikes, and drone attacks, as armed groups waged war for territorial control over strategic trafficking routes and illicit economies.
Meanwhile, 416 “humanitarian access restrictions” were registered, including threats and violence that impeded humanitarian personnel and their services to civilians – a 13% increase on 2024.
“In the past two years, the context has been characterised by the expansion and strengthening of Non-State Armed Groups (NSAG) in different territories of the country, an increase in territorial disputes… and in high-impact armed actions,” Yann Cornic, director of the Danish Refugee Council office in Colombia, told The New Humanitarian in an email exchange last month. “These factors have created greater risks for the affected communities and have significantly complicated the conditions for providing humanitarian assistance and protection.”
Who do aid agencies engage with?
Colombia has been gripped by a decades-long cycle of internal conflict, despite repeated efforts to address it. A landmark peace accord signed in 2016 led to the demobilisation of the FARC guerrilla group and a temporary lull, but new and more numerous armed groups filled the void and fighting has surged over the last decade.
During his 2022-2026 presidential term, De la Espriella’s predecessor, Gustavo Petro, sought to move away from predominantly military responses and pursue dialogue with armed groups through his so-called “Total Peace” plan. But the policy yielded little progress and a dramatic rise in violence has since triggered a cascade of new humanitarian needs.
Instead, De la Espriella, who took office on 7 August, has pledged to take a hardline stance against armed groups. He joined US President Donald Trump’s “Shield of the Americas” security alliance, and the US has pledged $1 billion in security assistance to Colombia.
De la Espriella has also vowed to restart Plan Colombia – a US-backed anti-drug military programme launched in 2000 that displaced hundreds of thousands of civilians – and to launch a new military offensive against armed groups. The latter is part of his so-called “Patriot Plan 2.0” – billed as a revamped version of an iron-fisted crackdown on armed groups launched in 2003 by Álvaro Uribe – a populist right-wing president.
Aid organisations worry that all this could bring a new escalation of violence, driving greater humanitarian needs and additional risks for their operations.
“For us, the situation will become much more difficult because the context will be harsher, and the [armed] groups will tighten their control [on territories],” said Etienne Malingrey, director in Colombia of the International NGO Safety Organization (INSO), which opened a new office in the country last year.
Experts say the fragmentation of armed groups has not only led to more fighting but also to changes in their structure and leadership. This, in turn, has made it more difficult for aid groups to identify who to engage with at the local level to obtain access to communities in need.
In the past, humanitarian organisations had to negotiate access with one or two main armed actors, but coordination is harder now that there are more. The rising number of ongoing conflicts and military operations only compounds that problem as it means more turnover in command positions within armed groups.
“It's becoming increasingly difficult to identify who's in charge within [armed] groups. Changes [in leadership] are happening much more quickly, the people involved are very young, and the operational chains aren't as clear,” explained Malingrey from INSO.
When they do engage with them, the risks are also higher, as this new generation of fighters often pays little heed to the underlying rules of humanitarian work. “These are people who are unfamiliar with international humanitarian law and human rights,” said Ángela Olaya, director of Conflict Response, a Bogotá-based organisation studying the armed conflict.
In this context, aid groups are often forced to call on community leaders to obtain access. But it is an approach that puts locals “at considerable risk”, and isn't necessarily efficient, Malingrey said. “The response is somewhat slow, and the reliability of the information we receive from leaders in certain contexts is also limited.”
As conflict deepens, mistrust grows
The past year has been particularly difficult.
As conflict has intensified, rival armed groups have sought to tighten their control over entire communities. In the Norte de Santander department, for instance, the National Liberation Army (ELN), one of the country´s main armed actors, started fighting a FARC dissident group in January 2025, displacing more than 138,000 people and confining over 12,000 over that year.
Constant ambushes, confrontations, and military operations have made armed groups increasingly suspicious of humanitarian actors.
“The command structures and internal cohesion within these groups are much more fragile and dynamic, the sense of mistrust toward everyone – including humanitarian actors – is pervasive,” Olaya explained.
As a result, many humanitarian access restrictions are related to interference with the movement of humanitarian personnel or agencies, with 51 cases reported between January and July 2026 – 20 more than in the same period of 2025.
“In Cauca, Norte de Santander, Bolívar, and Guaviare, the main issues or restrictions arise because a particular armed group is suspicious, imposes last-minute rules, or because… conflict actors [suddenly] become active, and it is no longer safe to provide assistance to the population,” said Cornic.
In March 2026, the Estado Mayor Central (EMC), a FARC dissident group present in at least 133 municipalities across the country, announced it was forbidding the UN Verification Mission, the Organisation of American States Mission to Support the Peace Process in Colombia (MAPP), and the Colombian Ombudsman’s office from carrying out activities in areas under their control. The group cited “violations of trust and neutrality”, claiming humanitarian assistance and verification missions had been used to gather intelligence to facilitate military operations against them.
The announcement was not the first of its kind.
In June 2025, a faction of the same group, the “Bloque Amazonas”, published a pamphlet “reminding” UN bodies and the MAPP that their access to areas under their control was restricted. A similar message was sent in Cauca in May 2025.
Barriers are sometimes more concrete. In Norte de Santander, humanitarian convoys have been forced to turn back several times when deploying in the field, with armed groups going as far as checking humanitarian staff´s personal phones. In southern Bolívar and the mountainous area of Nariño, some humanitarian teams have been asked to provide identification papers, letters of acceptance by community members, or access permits signed by armed groups.
The growing risks on the ground
Violence against healthcare workers has also been on the rise.
In February, a doctor and a nurse were kidnapped in Norte de Santander and forced to provide medical care to injured members of the ELN after a bombing by Colombia’s armed forces in the small town of Tibú.
A similar situation was reported in the violence-ridden southwestern department of Valle del Cauca in April. And in Briceño, in northwestern Antioquia department, an ambulance was stopped by members of an armed group who forced the medical personnel out of the vehicle and killed the patient.
According to data shared by the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC), there were 595 incidents of violence against medical missions in 2025 – 16 more than in 2024. Recorded incidents this year include threats against healthcare personnel, restrictions on access to services, demands for care under inadequate conditions, and obstruction of efforts to treat the wounded.
The theft of humanitarian vehicles is also becoming more common. At least six were reported stolen in 2025, and 10 so far in 2026, according to OCHA’s data. Armed groups use them for logistics but also because it allows them to move around under cover for criminal purposes.
“Between the reporting of the theft and the moment the truck is recovered [by the police]... there’s enough time for them to do what they need to do with it,” Olaya said.
Another source of concern is the greater use of drones carrying explosives. Although aid workers are not directly targeted, Malingrey said that there were five or six incidents last year in which humanitarian teams were on the ground when drones exploded only 300 to 500 metres away.
On 18 June, in Tibú, a drone attack during confrontations between armed groups forced a humanitarian organisation to suspend its activities and seek shelter. According to the Colombian Ministry of Defense, 333 attacks involving weaponised drones were registered in 2025, a 445% increase from 2024. OCHA recorded 158 drone attacks between January and July 2026, especially in Cauca, Norte de Santander, and Antioquia.
Aid groups are still looking for ways to cope with these new dynamics. In some areas, humanitarian workers have had to take alternative – often much longer – routes to avoid conflict zones or armed groups' checkpoints. They also avoid going to the field with personal phones.
Stijn Houben, head of operations at the ICRC’s regional delegation in Bogotá, said they are trying to adjust their strategies based on the experience of other countries.
“We’re making a huge change internally… where we learn best practices from other contexts that may be much more advanced in these issues – such as Ukraine – and try to adapt and implement them here for our teams,” he said.
Uncertainty about the impacts of the new government’s announced military offensive is already affecting humanitarian work. A staff member of one organisation who asked to speak anonymously because they weren’t at liberty to speak about this topic, said “armed groups don’t know what’s coming and are already increasing restrictions”.
“There’s more mistrust with humanitarian organisations too, so we’ve tightened our security and focused on building trust with communities,” they added. How all these complex dynamics will impact the response to the earthquake remains to be seen.
Edited by Daniela Mohor.

Jury in Luigi Mangione's insurance CEO murder trial to be anonymous
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:40:33 UTC
00:40:33 KST

The jury in Luigi Mangione's murder trial next month on charges of gunning down a health insurance executive on a Manhattan sidewalk will be anonymous, the judge overseeing the case said at a hearing on Tuesday.
New York State Justice Gregory Carro did not explain why jurors would remain anonymous. While not the norm in US criminal trials, judges often decide to shield jurors' identities from the public in high-profile cases where there is concern jurors may be subject to harassment or intimidation.
Mangione, 28, is accused of shooting UnitedHealthcare CEO Brian Thompson outside a hotel in December 2024. The killing was widely condemned by public officials but became emblematic of many Americans’ frustration with health insurance industry practices.
Mangione has pleaded not guilty to murder, weapons, and forgery charges brought by Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg. He also pleaded not guilty to stalking charges in a separate federal case.
At Tuesday's hearing, Carro also ruled that an overflow courtroom would be made available for the press and the public to witness the trial if the main courtroom fills to capacity.
Prosecutors say they can prove Mangione’s guilt with hundreds of hours of surveillance video, fingerprints, DNA, a cellphone, a gun with matching ballistics and a notebook in which Mangione allegedly wrote of hating insurers and wanting to kill.
Mangione’s lawyers have revealed almost nothing about their potential trial strategy, though a July court filing indicated they might argue Mangione lost control of his actions due to an extreme mental health breakdown. They have not conceded that he was the shooter.
Former UnitedHealthcare CEO shot dead outside hotel
Thompson led UnitedHealth Group's insurance unit before he was shot dead in the early morning outside a hotel where an investor conference was taking place.
Graphic footage of the killing and a five-day manhunt for a suspect made the case a media fixture and social media sensation. Mangione was arrested in Pennsylvania.
His trial in the federal case is scheduled to begin in January.

OurCrowd appoints Cali Chill as CEO after year as acting chief
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:40:16 UTC
00:40:16 KST

Venture capital firm OurCrowd has appointed Cali Chill as chief executive officer, formally placing the seven-year company veteran at the head of the Jerusalem-based venture investment platform after he served as acting CEO since mid-2025.
Chill had been serving as acting CEO and chief operating officer after OurCrowd founder Jon Medved stepped back from day-to-day management for medical reasons. The company’s board of directors has now appointed Chill to the role on a permanent basis, OurCrowd announced Tuesday.
“I am honored to lead the next chapter of the OurCrowd story,” Chill told The Jerusalem Post. “The company is made up of some extremely talented and hard-working people, and it will be thanks to their true dedication and unwavering commitment that we take the OurCrowd investment platform to new heights.”
Chill added that he was “energized by what we can all build together from here.”
Company working to introduce more AI tools into its investment and asset-management process
The formal appointment follows a year in which OurCrowd said its leadership team focused on stabilizing operations, narrowing its investment strategy toward fewer high-conviction deals and assessing options for monetizing parts of its existing portfolio. The company has also been working to introduce more artificial intelligence-based tools into its investment and asset-management processes.
Earlier this month, OurCrowd announced one of the largest exits in its portfolio after Visa agreed to acquire Israeli fraud-prevention company BioCatch in a deal reportedly valued at $2.4 billion.
OurCrowd was an early investor in BioCatch and continued investing in the company as it grew. The acquisition remains subject to regulatory approvals and other customary closing conditions and is expected to close by the end of Visa’s fiscal second quarter of 2027.
Ben Plotkin, chairman of OurCrowd’s board of directors, said the board had been encouraged by the company’s performance since Chill took over its day-to-day management.
'I am excited about OurCrowd’s prospects'
“I am very pleased with the company’s progress and accomplishments over the past several quarters,” Plotkin said. “Since Cali took charge of the operation, the firm has been managed with increasing financial discipline and operational focus.”
“Having worked closely with Cali since the leadership transition of 2025, I am excited about OurCrowd’s prospects, and the board has full confidence in Cali’s ability to lead the firm through this next chapter,” he added.
Chill joined OurCrowd seven years ago as general counsel and later served as chief legal officer. He subsequently became head of funds, overseeing the company’s in-house and third-party investment funds, before serving as chief investment officer and chairman of OurCrowd’s investment committee.
Before joining OurCrowd, Chill served as general counsel of Nasdaq-listed Answers Corporation and AFCV Holdings, a private-equity-backed technology investor.

Severe heatwaves in Bulgaria reveal ancient Rome's Constantine Bridge hidden under Danube's waters
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:40:01 UTC
00:40:01 KST

Exceptionally low water levels on the Danube have exposed parts of the foundations of the ancient Constantine Bridge, giving Bulgarian archaeologists a rare opportunity to document what they describe as one of the Roman Empire's most impressive engineering achievements.
A succession of record-breaking heatwaves in Europe this summer has caused a severe drought in parts of the continent, affecting major waterways such as the Danube and the Rhine.
"For archaeologists, these moments are extremely valuable because nature briefly reveals an object that is hidden beneath the Danube for most of the time," said Pavel Popov of the Regional Historical Museum in Pleven. "It gives us a rare opportunity to observe, document and study it in its real environment."
The bridge, commissioned by Emperor Constantine I and inaugurated in 328 CE, linked the ancient city of Ulpia Oescus with Sucidava in what is now Romania. Stretching more than two km (1.2 miles), it is regarded as one of the longest bridges of antiquity, though short-lived. It is believed to have been largely destroyed around 367 CE.
A team from the Pleven museum carried out aerial photography and documentation of visible bridge remains, close to the village of Gigen in northern Bulgaria.
Researchers used drones to map and photograph the exposed remains, tracing the line of visible foundations and assessing their condition.
The Danube's water levels have been receding all across Europe, revealing finds from as far back as the Ice Age.
Near the Serbian port of Prahovo, the Danube’s receding water levels have revealed dozens of German battleships sunken during World War II.
At least 20 warships can be seen emerging from the Danube, according to CBS News, adding that they are believed to have belonged to Nazi Germany’s Black Sea fleet, sunken by the German’s during their retreat from Romania near the end of the war.
Remains of two Nazi soldiers found in Budapest
On the riverbank near Batthyány Square in Budapest, the German War Graves Commission (Volksbund) announced last week that the skeletal remains of two Nazi soldiers had been found alongside a World War II DKW NZ 350-1 motorcycle.
The dog tags of both soldiers are complete, Volksbund noted, explaining that this likely meant the two soldiers had been declared missing as no offical notice of their deaths had been recorded at the time.
The two are expected to be buried in the Budaörs German Soldiers Cemetery outside of Budapest.
Ice Age mammoth remains unearthed in Bulgaria
Two weeks ago, the Bulgarian News Agency (BTA) reported that the remains of the ancient mammoth (Mammuthus primigenius) were found in northern Bulgaria after Danube's water levels receded to a record low.
The animal's remains were found by a local resident of the Ryahovo village, who noticed the unusual find on the river bed. Villagers then alerted specialists in the Regional History Museum in the nearby town of Ruse.
Ryahovo Mayor Hristina Ivanova shared with BTA that the area was particularly interesting from a historical perspective, and that over the years, “various finds have been discovered here, including animal remains, vessels and other objects.”
Miriam Sela-Eitam contributed to this report.

Chinese citizen pleads guilty to attempting to smuggle US military equipment to PRC
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:40:01 UTC
00:40:01 KST

A Chinese citizen pleaded guilty in federal court to charges related to attempting to smuggle US military hardware to China in violation of the US Arms Export Control Act, according to the US Justice Department (DOJ) on Monday.
Dingwei Chen, a 29-year-old citizen of the People's Republic of China (PRC), tried to purchase US-manufactured military-grade communications hardware, such as radios and satellite modems, on the black market.
Chen worked with individuals in China to acquire the communications systems from foreign arms dealers, eventually making a down payment of $40,000 to secure the technology.
The co-conspirators then made another payment in cryptocurrency, equivalent to $30,000, for the purchase of 10 satellite modems.
Chen, co-conspirators attempted to avoid law enforcement detection
Chen and his accomplices shifted to using cryptocurrency because, according to the DOJ, they noted that cryptocurrency wallets “are essentially anonymous bank accounts. Each transaction processed through them is private and untraceable.”
Using encrypted apps to communicate in an effort to avoid detection, Chen claimed that the purchase of 10 modems was just an initial deal, and that he had enough funding to obtain tens of millions of dollars worth of military technology.
Chen and the co-conspirators also discussed plans to covertly export the hardware to China, initially weighing shipping them through Switzerland or retrieving them in Saipan in the Northern Mariana Islands. Eventually, they agreed to smuggle the military equipment through Mexico.
Such hardware is illegal to export without a license issued by the US Department of State’s Directorate of Defense Trade Controls, the DOJ stated.
Chen’s guilty plea was accepted by a federal court in Salt Lake City, and he faces a maximum sentence of 20 years in prison.
Smuggled equipment may have been used against US
Assistant Attorney General for National Security John A. Eisenberg asserted that if Chen had been successful in diverting the communications equipment to China, it may have been used against the US in the future.
“Today’s guilty plea underscores the serious consequences for those who attempt to illegally procure, export, or transfer sensitive US-origin military equipment,” Acting Special Agent in Charge of US Immigration and Customs Enforcement Investigations Spiros Karabinas added.
“Protecting American innovation and preventing critical technologies from reaching foreign adversaries, terrorist organizations, and transnational criminal organizations is a core national security mission,” Karabinas continued, praising law enforcement agencies for their collaboration on holding Chen accountable.

Even the AI assistant inside Russia’s state-backed Max messenger tells users not to trust the app
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

15:39:30 UTC
00:39:30 KST

Even the AI assistant inside Russia’s state-backed Max messenger tells users not to trust the app
Alisa, the AI assistant built into the Max messenger, has been telling users in chat conversations to install other apps instead — Telegram, Signal, or WhatsApp. The independent Russian news outlet Verstka noticed the pattern.
Asked about Max’s security, the AI — developed by Yandex — cites several vulnerabilities: it lacks end-to-end encryption, for instance, and routes user data to servers owned by VK.
“The messenger collects IP addresses, activity times, contacts, device and browser type, information about the provider, and other metadata. This data may be shared with third parties, including government agencies, upon a lawful request (for example, a court order or a motivated request from authorized bodies),” the AI warns.
Alisa recommends keeping Max on a separate device — a “maxphone” — rather than installing it on the phone you use for banking apps, private chats, and other personal information you want to keep from the government.
If Max must be used on a personal phone, Alisa suggests relying on the messenger’s browser version and denying the app permanent access to location, contacts, microphone, and camera.

How to protect your eyes while watching the solar eclipse
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:36:41 UTC
00:36:41 KST

As thousands of people prepare to watch a solar eclipse on 12 August, eye-health experts are urging viewers to use proper protective eyewear to avoid potentially serious damage to their vision.
Europe is set to witness a rare celestial event on August 12, when the Moon will pass between the Earth and the Sun, casting its shadow across parts of the continent.
The total eclipse will be visible in Greenland, Iceland, Spain and a small area of northeastern Portugal. Other parts of Europe will experience a partial solar eclipse.
In Spain, the eclipse will cross the country from west to east before continuing over the Balearic Islands. It will be the first total solar eclipse visible from mainland Spain since 1905 and the first of three total solar eclipses that will be visible from the country between 2026 and 2028.
“This kind of eclipse happens quite often on Earth, but at different places. However, it happens in a specific place only every 400 years,” Philippe Escoubet, Space Plasma Scientist at the European Space Agency_,_ told Euronews. “So if you have an eclipse passing by your village or your city, it will not come back [for] 400 years, so it's almost a lifetime event that you want to see.”
But watching the eclipse safely requires proper eye protection.
What glasses should you use?
For those who are going to watch it, eye health experts remind people to wear proper glasses, which comply with the ISO 12312-2 international safety standard.
Gabriëlle Janssen, president of the European Council of Optometry and Optics (ECOO), told Euronews that consumers should not rely solely on the ISO number printed on a product.
“The ISO number printed on the glasses does not by itself guarantee that they have been properly tested,” she said.
Janssen advised viewers to buy eclipse glasses from a reputable supplier and check them for scratches, holes or other damage before use.
The glasses should be put on before looking towards the Sun and removed only after looking away from it.
“Children should be supervised carefully,” she said.
People who do not have suitable eclipse glasses should not look directly at the Sun. Instead, they can follow the eclipse through a livestream, such as one provided by the European Southern Observatory (ESO).
Ordinary sunglasses are not a safe alternative, even if they appear very dark, as they "do not provide sufficient protection against the intense visible, ultraviolet and infrared radiation from the Sun,” Janssen said.
What about cameras, binoculars and telescopes?
Looking at the Sun through a camera, binoculars or telescope can also be dangerous unless the equipment has been fitted with a specially designed solar filter.
“Wearing eclipse glasses while looking through these devices is not sufficient, because the concentrated sunlight can damage both the filter and the eyes,” Janssen said.
Older methods sometimes suggested for viewing eclipses, including CDs and photographic negatives, should also be avoided.
“They don't block enough [light], and then some light is passing by. This can be very dangerous for your eyes,” Escoubet said.
Why is it important to protect the eyes?
Although the total phase of an eclipse lasts only a few minutes, looking directly at the Sun can cause permanent damage to the retina – a thin, light-sensitive layer of tissue that lines the back inner part of the eye.
Looking directly at the Sun can cause solar retinopathy, a photochemical injury to the retinal tissue. The macula, the part of the retina responsible for sharp, detailed central vision, is particularly vulnerable.
“The retina has no pain receptors. This means that the damage can occur without pain and without the person immediately realising that anything is wrong,” Janssen said, adding that symptoms often only become noticeable several hours later.
Symptoms of solar retinopathy can include blurred or reduced central vision; a dark, blurred or blind spot in the centre of the visual field; distorted vision, with straight lines appearing bent; changes in colour perception; and increased sensitivity to light.
“Anyone who notices blurred, reduced or distorted vision after looking at the Sun should avoid any further exposure and seek prompt assessment from an optometrist, ophthalmologist or other appropriate eye-care professional,” Janssen said.
While those with mild damage usually “experience gradual improvement over the following weeks or months,” full recovery “is not guaranteed,” she said.
A central blind spot, reduced visual acuity or distorted vision can persist if retinal cells are permanently damaged, she said, adding that “currently no proven treatment that can reverse solar retinal damage” exists.
“Prevention through correct eye protection is therefore essential,” she said.

Ukraine to rebury another Nazi collaborator
Unknown

2026-08-11
15:35:28 UTC

Ukraine to rebury another Nazi collaborator
Ukraine has exhumed the remains of Evgeny Konovalets, founder of the Organization of Ukrainian Nationalists (OUN) that collaborated with Nazi Germany, from a cemetery in the Netherlands for reburial as a national hero, Vladimir Zelensky’s office has announced.
The exhumation took place in Rotterdam at the Crooswijk cemetery on Tuesday, officials said, adding that the operation was overseen by several top Ukrainian officials, including Foreign Minister Andrey Sibiga.
Konovalets’ remains are to be temporarily reburied at Ukraine’s National Military Memorial Cemetery, with a farewell ceremony planned at a Kiev cathedral, the statement added.
Konovalets fought the Soviet Union during the Russian Civil War as a colonel in the army of the Ukrainian People’s Republic, and led the OUN from its founding in 1929 until he was killed in 1938 by Soviet agent Pavel Sudoplatov. According to declassified documents, a group of Ukrainian nationalists led by Konovalets – codenamed “Consul I” – had supplied German intelligence with data on Poland from 1925 and moved into “espionage and sabotage” work inside the Soviet Union after 1939.
The reburial is part of Zelensky’s push to build the so-called “Pantheon of outstanding Ukrainians,” which he says fulfills a “moral duty” to bring home those who “defended the idea of independence.” Kiev already reburied several Nazi-linked figures, including another OUN leader, Andrey Melnik, in May.
Russian Foreign Ministry spokeswoman Maria Zakharova previously blasted efforts to rebury Konovalets, saying Zelensky “proudly calls them [OUN leaders] ‘heroes and freedom fighters,’” while “cynically turning a blind eye to the crimes of those butchers.” She also accused Zelensky of “dancing on the bones” of those who fell victim to the OUN, once again confirming the “misanthropic essence” of his government.
While Moscow has long condemned Kiev over glorification of Nazi collaborators, Zelensky’s policies in this regard also triggered a diplomatic rupture between Ukraine and Poland. The row erupted after Zelensky decided to name a special forces unit “Heroes of the UPA,” in honor of the Ukrainian Insurgent Army, which killed more than 100,000 Poles during the 1943–1945 Volhynia massacres.

In 5 questions: What does Türkiye's National Solidarity bill entail?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:35:21 UTC
00:35:21 KST

Turkish parliament has passed the Bill on Strengthening National Solidarity and Social Cohesion, marking the first legislative step under the country's "Terror-Free Türkiye" initiative.
Lawmakers approved the legislation on Monday following approximately 12 hours of debate, with 467 voting in favor — nearly four-fifths of parliament's 600 members — reflecting broad political consensus behind the process.
The bill establishes a conditional legal framework for certain investigations, prosecutions, and the enforcement of prison sentences after the verified dissolution and disarmament of the PKK/KCK terrorist organization and its affiliated structures.
Its explanatory rationale stresses that the measure does not constitute an amnesty, abolish convictions, or terminate criminal liability, describing it instead as a "limited and conditional" arrangement within the criminal justice and penal system.
Here is what the legislation provides — and how the mechanism is expected to work:
Parliament's passage of the bill does not by itself activate its core legal measures.
Türkiye's security institutions must first determine that the PKK/KCK and its affiliated structures have ended their activities and surrendered all weapons and ammunition under their control.
The National Security Council (MGK) must then confirm that determination, with the decision published in the Official Gazette.
Only after that stage can eligible individuals apply to benefit from the measures under the legislation.
Applications must be submitted in writing within six months of publication of the MGK decision to a chief public prosecutor's office or an institution designated by the oversight board established under the legislation.
The bill covers offenses related to establishing or leading the PKK/KCK, membership in the organization, knowingly assisting it, disseminating its propaganda, committing crimes as part of its activities and terrorism-financing offenses committed for its benefit.
For investigations and prosecutions involving offenses punishable by up to 15 years in prison, proceedings may be deferred for five years.
For offenses carrying more than 15 years in prison, life imprisonment or aggravated life imprisonment, the deferral period is 10 years.
Similar periods apply to the enforcement of finalized prison sentences: five years for total sentences of up to 15 years and 10 years for sentences exceeding 15 years, including life and aggravated life imprisonment.
The mechanism does not apply to intentional killings committed as part of the organization's activities. Certain offenses committed before June 1, 2005, are also excluded.
The rationale explicitly says the measure does not abolish criminal convictions, change the legal character of offenses, or terminate criminal liability.
Instead, investigations, prosecutions, and finalized convictions remain legally in place while proceedings or enforcement are deferred under specified conditions.
If an individual commits another terrorism-related offense during the deferral period, the measure is revoked and criminal proceedings or enforcement of the sentence resumes.
If no such offense is committed, an investigation or prosecution ultimately ends with a decision not to prosecute or dismissal, while a finalized prison sentence is deemed served once the relevant deferral period expires.
The rationale therefore describes the legislation as a "limited and conditional statutory arrangement" concerning the criminal justice and penal system rather than an amnesty.
The bill establishes an oversight board chaired by Türkiye's vice president.
Its members include the ministers of justice, foreign affairs, interior and national defense, the secretary-general of the Presidency, the head of the National Intelligence Organization (MIT) and the secretary-general of the National Security Council.
The board will monitor implementation, periodically assess whether the organization has fully dissolved based on observation reports and may seek additional judicial, administrative, or legislative measures when necessary.
It will also periodically review decisions made under the framework.
Parliament will retain an oversight role. The board will regularly brief lawmakers, while a parliamentary Monitoring Commission will follow implementation and issue recommendations.
With parliament having approved the legislation, attention now shifts from the legislative process to verification and implementation.
The core mechanisms under the legislation depend on security institutions determining that the PKK/KCK and its affiliated structures have ended their activities and surrendered their weapons, followed by confirmation from the National Security Council and publication of its decision in the Official Gazette.
Only then will the application process and the provisions concerning investigations, prosecutions, and prison sentences become operational.
The legislation may also not be the final legislative measure under the broader process. Its explanatory rationale says additional amendments or new legislation could be introduced depending on emerging needs, accumulated experience, and changing circumstances.

UN officials: Occupied West Bank at 'breaking point'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:35:18 UTC
00:35:18 KST

Yasin Gungor
11 August 2026•Update: 11 August 2026
UN officials said Tuesday that the occupied West Bank has reached a critical "breaking point," citing a record surge in violence by Israeli occupiers and a systematic unraveling of Palestinian statehood.
“Humanitarian organizations cannot create the political conditions that children need to be safe,” UNICEF Deputy Executive Director Hannan Sulieman told the Security Council. "That responsibility rests with member states who have the power to change them.”
She presented four urgent requests to the international community: prioritizing the protection of children by ending administrative detention, halting forced displacements through diplomatic pressure, ensuring safe access to schools and hospitals, and allowing vital humanitarian supplies to move without delay.
Sulieman reported a harrowing toll on minors, noting that 174 Palestinian children have been verified killed in the occupied West Bank since January 2024. “That is more than one child every week,” she noted.
She revealed that at least 355 Palestinian children are being held in military detention, the highest in eight years, with nearly half detained without charge or trial.
Systematic displacement
Deputy Special Coordinator Ramiz Alakbarov echoed the alarm, describing the situation as an emergency requiring immediate action to prevent de facto annexation. He said that in 2026, dozens of Palestinians have been killed and approximately 3,800 displaced due to demolitions, evictions and attacks by occupiers.
“Settler violence has reached unprecedented levels,” Alakbarov emphasized, citing more than 1,400 documented incidents this year affecting 260 communities. He warned of a clear pattern where Palestinian populations are forcibly removed from their villages before occupiers seize the vacated land. The official noted that for the first time, land was explicitly seized in "Area A" to build infrastructure for occupiers.
Fiscal, political instability
Alakbarov also highlighted the acute fiscal crisis facing the Palestinian Authority (PA), which reportedly lacks $6 billion in clearance revenues withheld by Israeli authorities. Amid the turmoil, he welcomed preparations for legislative elections scheduled for Nov. 28.
Alakbarov concluded that they are "interconnected steps, not isolated developments," aimed at weakening Palestinian governance.
He said if the situation continues to unravel, it will undermine implementation of resolution 2803 (2025) and erode the prospects for realizing a negotiated Israeli-Palestinian peace based on the two-state solution, urging an immediate “and in full" implementation of resolution 2803, together with the New York Declaration.
Resolution 2803 endorses a peace plan proposed by US President Donald Trump for the Gaza Strip and a temporary international force in the enclave following two years of war.
Since the start of Israel's genocide in Gaza on Oct. 8, 2023, Israeli forces and occupiers have escalated attacks in the West Bank, killing more than 1,183 Palestinians, injuring 13,000 and arresting nearly 25,000, according to Palestinian figures.
Palestinians warn that the escalation is part of Israeli efforts to impose facts on the ground, paving the way for annexation of the occupied West Bank and undermining the possibility of establishing a Palestinian state along the 1967 borders under relevant UN resolutions.

The administration is taking the necessary actions to maintain law and order. We agreed to everything that was within the state government's capacity to address."
She said cancellation of the CGL examination involved legal considerations. "The issue they are adamant about cancelling the CGL exam- involves judicial directives. The students should understand that the state government must operate within the framework of constitutional rights and established guidelines. We have accepted nearly every point," Pandey Singh added.She said the government was open to investigations into alleged financial irregularities."We have kept the investigation open-ended; the state government is ready to have the probe conducted under judicial monitoring. We have even invited the Enforcement Directorate (ED) to investigate the allegations of financial misappropriation," she said.

Estonian yacht NOLA wins class at prestigious Copa del Rey regatta
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:31:00 UTC
00:31:00 KST

Estonian yacht NOLA wins class at prestigious Copa del Rey regatta
An Estonian-owned yacht won its class at a prestigious regatta off Mallorca, a race which also saw Spanish royalty competing.
The yacht, the NOLA, a Carkeek 40 type, won the Sail Racing ORC A class at the 44th Copa del Rey MAPFRE regatta, and its crew of Margus Uudam and Taavet Hinrikus edged out two-time ORC world champion Rán in a battle decided in the final race.
The Copa del Rey, held in Palma Bay, is one of Europe's most prestigious regattas, bringing together nearly a hundred yachts along with numerous world and European champions. Spain's King Felipe VI also competed, sailing on the navy yacht Hispania.
"The feeling is unbelievable and very intense. Each day felt like a new competition. Nothing came easy," said co-owner Uudam after the win.
Hinrikus described it as a second-by-second battle: "Rán has been practically unbeatable for years, and even they said finally someone had come along with whom they could really race for the win."
The team's main rival was Niklas Zennström's Rán, winner of the ORC A world title in 2025 and 2026.
The NOLA's journey began in 2021 as an amateur team, and today the boat brings together professionals and amateurs. "We started purely as amateurs. Now we have changed boats and the team includes both. We want to continue with this model – to work as one team with high goals," said Uudam.
The NOLA's tactics were spearheaded by crew member Hugo Rocha, a Portuguese Olympic bronze medallist, and Norwegian navigator Aksel Magdahl as. Rocha, for whom this was his fourth Copa del Rey class win, highlighted the teamwork and intense competition with Rán throughout the week.
On the opening day, the NOLA took one race win and one second place. On day two, a 32-nautical-mile coastal race saw Magdahl and Rocha's bold route choice give the NOLA a convincing win, lifting the team to sole leader in its class.
The decisive race, held in light and changeable winds, turned into a serious duel. The NOLA held its position and secured victory in the class, also receiving the overall winner's trophy.
The NOLA has been a serious competitor for the top for a long time, and it's a huge achievement to make it all the way now," Uudam added. "We got a good start and had to give our best both tactically and technically in very light conditions. Everything went well. This is our first major regatta win, an important milestone, and gives us confidence for the future," he went on.
The win continues Estonia's strong offshore sailing results in Palma Bay. Last year, Mati Sepp's Technonicol won the ORC B European title at the same regatta.

EU's Taliban tension laid bare five years after Afghanistan takeover
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:30:56 UTC
00:30:56 KST

Exclusive: Five years after the Taliban seized power in Afghanistan, former women’s affairs minister Sami Samar tells Euronews she is disappointed by the EU’s engagement with a regime overseeing a "human rights disaster."
When news broke that members of the Taliban visited Brussels in late June to discuss migration with European Union officials, Sami Samar said she was left crestfallen.
“I was so disappointed because we really count on European countries as they believe the principle of human rights and the universality of human rights,” she tells Euronews.
“This is kind of (an) endorsement of the Taliban, even if it's low rank of the Taliban.”
Samar, a former Afghan minister for women’s affairs from 2001 to 2002 turned human rights advocate living in exile in the United States, made the comments in the days leading up to 15 August 2026.
This is five years since the Taliban established itself as the de facto government of Afghanistan, which only Russia formally recognises. This date also marks the beginning of when the US withdrew its troops from the landlocked South Asian country after maintaining a presence there for nearly 20 years.
In the half-decade since this date, the country plunged into crisis.
The International Committee of the Red Cross says Afghanistan’s humanitarian situation remains among the most severe in the world.
The United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs estimates roughly 21.9 million people (45% of the total population) are projected to require humanitarian assistance in 2026.
This is due to years of armed conflict, economic fragility, underinvestment in basic services and the rapid attrition of rights.
Women and girls
Samar said life has deteriorated in Afghanistan as the Taliban’s grip on power has tightened.
“It’s a human rights disaster,” she said, adding that women and girls are particularly impacted with more than 160 decrees issued "reducing” their lives.
This includes banning girls from education beyond sixth grade, banning women from university and excluding women from most workplaces. It also spans imposing strict dress codes and restricting women and girls from travelling without a male guardian.
Human Rights Watch states these policies amount to crimes against humanity through gender persecution.
“They are putting more restrictions on women's freedom and movement, and violating women's rights, including women's voice should not be heard. Can you imagine that you cannot even cry loudly?” Samar said.
A majority of members of the European Parliament in May condemned the restrictions of the rights of girls, women and other minorities in the country, as well as public floggings and executions.
The MEPs also called on the European Commission and member states to maintain non-recognition and non-normalisation of the Taliban regime, while also laying bare their “regret” the decision to invite the Taliban to the Belgian capital.
European Commission spokesperson Anitta Hipper said on Tuesday the EU remains “gravely concerned” about systematic human rights violations in Afghanistan five-years on from the Taliban sweeping Kabul and a mantle of authority.
“The EU continues to call on the Afghan de facto authorities to respect, protect and fulfil the human rights and freedoms of all Afghans and to abide by Afghanistan's international obligation,” she said.
Hipper said member states in 2021 mandated that the EU executive maintain “operational engagement” with the de facto Afghan authorities.
“That being said, we do not recognise such or legitimise the authorities, but we speak with Taliban on topics that we consider our key values and interests,” she added.
These benchmarks span the condemnation of human rights violations in Afghanistan — such as gender-based discrimination — and standing ready to adopt targeted restrictive measures against those responsible for these violations and abuses.
Samar said these comments, which come months after earlier talks with European Commission officials and members of the Taliban on European soil, reveal a “double standard” by the bloc.
“There is a slight, very light condemnation of the situation of human rights in Afghanistan, but then you receive their delegation quietly?” she said.
“How can we trust these partnerships?”
Meeting about migration
The closed-door migration talks were conducted by officials from the European Commission and 15 member states as well as a delegation of Taliban representatives on 23 June. They occurred off official EU premises.
The Taliban stated the technical talks concerned the return of Afghan nationals without a right to stay in Europe, while the European Commission stressed it was about expediting returns involving people convicted of serious crimes or considered security threats.
Despite the scope, it drew fierce condemnation. UN High Commissioner for Human Rights Volker Türk told Euronews he was concerned about Afghan nationals being deported into a situation where “they may be tortured.”
Swedish Migration Minister Johan Forssell told local media, however, that it is “incredibly important” these individuals are deported.
"And that is not possible today. They do not want to participate. They do not want to go home,” he said.
In Sweden, roughly 200 Afghan nationals are awaiting deportation having been convicted of serious crimes, such as aggravated rape and aggravated drug trafficking, Forssell explained.
Amid the criticism, he argued that his government must negotiate with dictatorships in certain situations to "protect Swedish interests."
Deportations have proven particularly difficult for Afghans, who were among the top nationalities served with return orders in 2025.
According to Eurostat, out of the 14,270 Afghan nationals ordered to leave the bloc in the first nine months of last year, only 340 were sent back, representing a 2% return rate.
Meanwhile, arrivals into Europe continue.
More than 3,300 Afghans irregularly crossed EU borders in the first four months of 2026, mostly through the Eastern Mediterranean route, and over 63,000 Afghan nationals sought asylum in the EU in 2025, 10% of the overall number of asylum seekers.
According to Samar, these individuals are illegally entering the EU as the legal pathways to obtain a visa are onerous and because remaining in Afghanistan is perilous.
“Because of the dire situation that they face in Afghanistan, they try to cross the border," she said.

Russian anti-war party’s election ban dismays some voters

Russian anti-war party’s election ban dismays some voters
Aug 11 - Some voters and supporters of Russia's only registered anti-war party on Tuesday criticised a Supreme Court ruling to exclude it from the upcoming parliamentary election.
With only a handful of regional seats, Yabloko was never going to dent the dominance of the Putin-backed United Russia party in the September vote, but Monday’s court decision further limits Russians’ ability to register their opposition to the four-year war at a time when any dissent carries risks.
Yabloko has vowed to appeal the verdict, thanking backers for their support in a post on the Telegram messaging app that had attracted over 100,000 likes as of Tuesday afternoon.
"It was wrong to bar them. Why not give them the opportunity to (put forward) some of their own plans and ideas?" said Alla Galieva, who was visiting Moscow from her hometown of Barnaul in western Siberia.
Yabloko, once a leading liberal force in post-Soviet Russia, denies the allegations in the lawsuit upheld by the court, including that it received undeclared campaign support from Western sources.
It had used its campaign platform to promote its call for Russia to sign a ceasefire agreement with Ukraine, providing a rare outlet for the expression of anti-war views that the authorities have sought to stifle with tightened censorship laws, rights groups say.
"Democracy should not be restricted in this way. I'd prefer if everyone who wants to should be able to put forward their programme and try to run in an election on equal terms," said Moscow resident Tatyana Uvarova, on a leafy thoroughfare of the capital. "But what can be done? These are difficult times."
Another passerby, who gave his name as Daniil, said he was not sure who to vote for if Yabloko remained out of the running.
"The anti-war majority of Russians saw that it had a unique chance to express its position and vote for an anti-war party. The Kremlin could not allow that," said Yulia Navalnaya, the widow of late Russian dissident Alexei Navalny, in a video posted online.
The level of war fatigue and anti-war sentiment is difficult to assess due to strict state controls on dissent.
Intensifying Ukrainian drone attacks on Russian energy facilities and logistics hubs have disrupted daily life in many parts of the country in recent months, prompting the authorities to intervene to support the domestic fuel market. REUTERS

UK set to record 'warmest summer on record' as Europe preps for yet another heatwave
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:29:25 UTC
00:29:25 KST

"These statistics are a clear reminder – if one were needed – of the stark changes that we are seeing in the UK’s climate," senior Met Office scientist Mike Kendon said.
The United Kingdom is on course to record its "warmest summer on record," according to the country's national weather service.
Provisional statistics released by the Met Office on Tuesday showed that the current recorded mean temperature across the UK from 1 June to 10 August was 16.48°C - 1.88°C above the average for 1991-2020.
"To equal the current UK summer mean temperature record of 16.12°C set in 2025, the remainder of August would only need to average 0.21°C below the 1991-2020 long-term average for meteorological summer," the Met Office said. "Any warmer than this would establish a new UK record, with records starting in 1884".
In England, the mean summer temperature has so far clocked in at 18.19°C over the same period - up 2.46°C on the average - meaning the rest of the month would need to be 1.41°C below the nation's summer average to equal the current record, which was also set in 2025, per the Met Office.
A record-breaking summer is also looking likely in Wales, which has recorded a mean summer temperature of 16.8°C so far, 2.17°C above average.
Mike Kendon, a senior scientist with the Met Office, said: "Another record-breaking summer now looks increasingly likely for the UK, following the second-warmest June, and second-warmest July on record and yet another heatwave imminent."
"These statistics are a clear reminder – if one were needed – of the stark changes that we are seeing in the UK’s climate," he added.
It comes as the Met Office issued amber heat alerts for parts of the UK on Thursday, with temperatures expected to reach up to 37°C in some areas.
Western Europe is also gearing up to face yet another heatwave this week.
In France, 43 departments are under an orange heat alert on Tuesday. That number will rise to 78 on Wednesday as the heat intensifies, with temperatures of up to 40°C forecast.
Much of Spain is also facing weather warnings, with sweltering conditions potentially exceeding 40°C in parts of the country.
The brutal weather has put increasing strain on emergency services across the continent in recent months, while also helping the spread of a series of deadly wildfires and severe droughts.
There has also been an uptick in excess deaths linked to the heatwaves. In England, a report by the UK Health Security Agency showed there were 2,877 excess deaths caused by heatwaves in May and June.

====================================================================================================

Russia to retain forces in Syria as Hmeimim, Tartous become joint training centers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:25:20 UTC
00:25:20 KST

Russia will retain a military presence in Syria under a new agreement that will transform its longtime bases at Hmeimim and Tartous into joint training centers, while civilian facilities at the sites are transferred to Syrian control, officials and a Syrian source said Tuesday.
Syria and Russia signed a memorandum of understanding on Aug. 9 settling the future of Moscow’s military bases after 18 months of negotiations, according to Syria’s state news agency, citing the Syrian Foreign Ministry. The fate of the installations had been uncertain since longtime Russian ally Bashar Assad fled Syria in 2024.
Under the agreement, the Syrian state will assume management of civilian infrastructure, including Hmeimim airport and the commercial berth at Tartous port, allowing the facilities to be gradually incorporated into Syria’s civilian administration.
Military facilities at the two sites will meanwhile be converted into joint training centers, with the transition expected to be completed within three months.
A Syrian source familiar with the agreement said Russian forces would continue to be stationed at the new training centers, although the source did not provide details on troop numbers.
Russia’s Foreign Ministry hailed the agreement as an important step in relations between Moscow and Damascus.
“We regard the signing on 9 August of the memorandum between the Russian Federation and the Syrian Arab Republic on the operation of our bases in Hmeimim and Tartous as an important step aimed at further improving bilateral cooperation in the military sphere,” the ministry said.
“We believe that reaching this agreement will give further impetus to the development of the full range of relations between our countries, which are based on a long history of friendship and partnership,” it added.
The agreement allows Moscow to preserve at least part of the military infrastructure that has underpinned its influence in Syria and the wider region for years.
Russia views the bases as important assets for projecting power across the Middle East, the Mediterranean and Africa. Tartous is Russia’s only Mediterranean repair and replenishment facility, while Syria has also served as a staging point for Russian military contractors traveling to and from Africa.
Moscow granted asylum to Assad and his family after he fled the country.
A Syrian court on Tuesday sentenced Assad to death following a trial in absentia, convicting him of crimes including killings, torture and arbitrary arrest during Syria’s nearly 14-year war.
Separately, Russia is also preparing to restore part of its civilian presence in Damascus. State news agency TASS reported that Moscow will reopen its cultural center in the Syrian capital, known as the Russian House, following talks between a Russian delegation and Syrian officials over the weekend.
The military agreement suggests that despite Assad’s removal, Damascus and Moscow are seeking to preserve a working relationship while redefining the Russian role in Syria. The transfer of civilian facilities gives Syrian authorities greater formal control, while the continued presence of Russian personnel ensures Moscow retains a strategic foothold at two of the country’s most important military sites.

Probation officers vote for strike action after warning early releases will add to excessive workloads - UK politics live
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
15:23:23 UTC
00:23:23 KST

Probation officers vote overwhelmingly for strike action, after warning early releases will add to excessive workloads
Probation officers have voted overwhelmingly in favour of potential strike action over excessive workloads – a problem they expect to get worse when the next prisoner early release scheme starts in the autumn.
This ballot wasn’t about members being too busy. It was professionals warning they cannot protect the public properly when there are not enough people to do the work.
Probation staff make decisions every day with life-changing consequences for the people we work with, victims and the public. If excessive workloads prevent those decisions being made properly, the consequences can be devastating.
The government cannot keep treating probation as the place where the consequences of failures elsewhere in the criminal justice system are dumped. If you overload probation, you increase risk. It really is that simple.
The strike ballot was not directly related to the early release scheme. The union is demanding various measures to reduce workload, summarised as seven key demands. But it has called for more offenders to be exempt from the early release scheme, saying that is necessary to “ensure public protection is achievable”.
It is not clear if the prime minister or the lord chancellor have a clue about how ill-equipped the probation service is take on this additional work. For example, there are huge numbers of vacancies in victim liaison roles, so we are not sure how the extra victim contact scheme work will be done.
Plans to release thousands of domestic abuse perpetrators and stalkers early from prison does not boost victim protection. It means that more people, largely women, will be killed or harmed.
There’s no additional funding to retain probation staff, especially experienced ones, despite endemic staffing problems in many key roles across England and Wales.
Whilst it is welcome that the prime minister has intervened to review the policy, more now needs to be done to ensure the probation service can safely manage people in the community.
Commenting on the strike ballot, a Ministry of Justice spokesperson said:
Our probation staff do a vital job, working tirelessly to cut crime and keep the public safe. That is why we secured the biggest probation pay increase in over a decade in April and are working to cut down workloads by recruiting 1,300 probation officers this year and investing in technology that can drive down admin workloads.
While this ballot is only indicative, we are disappointed with the outcome and will continue to engage with Napo and other recognised trade unions.
38% of voters think Burnham's government will be better than Starmer's, and only 6% think it will be worse, poll suggests
Polling is pretty good for Andy Burnham at the moment (see 11.23am), and Lord Ashcroft has released a new polling report that confirms that voters are reasonably positive about the new government.
It suggests 38% of people think Burnham’s government will be different from Keir Starmer’s “in a good way” and only 6% think the opposite.
Fraser Nelson, the Times columnist and former Spectator editor, has a good post on his Substack blog about Zia Yusuf’s rant about Ben Wallace (see 3.22pm), and about the danger his extremism poses to Nigel Farage and Reform UK generally.
Nelson suggests he agrees with Tim Montgomerie, the ConservativeHome founder who defected to Reform UK and who is now a high-profile voice on the more Tory-friendly wing of the party. Montgomerie posted this about Yusuf last night.
This isn’t acceptable from Zia.
I don’t agree with @BenWallace70 on this issue but Zia’s language and attitude towards any opponent is inappropriate and counter-productive. Opponents are opponents... they aren’t enemies... or “moron”s.
One of the biggest question marks over @Nigel_Farage‘s leadership is whether he brings Zia under control. ZY is a man of huge talents but too much of his social media output is intemperate and reinforces Reform’s number one problem: our negative tactical voting challenge.
And here’s an extract from Nelson’s article.
The tactical voting is the issue: Reform has moved so far to the authoritarian end of the spectrum (it’s not a move to the right) that there are people on the right, left and centre now united in their main political desire: to vote for whoever, locally, will stop Reform. They’d rather see Andy Burnham in No 10 than someone like Yusuf in the Home Office.
Montgomerie’s post says what a lot of Reform supporters think: that Yusuf, for all his eloquence and talents, now looks out of control. And is starting to damage a party that needs to build alliances if it stands any chance of power. Some Tories even joke that Yusuf (a former Tory member) is a sleeper agent. So why doesn’t Farage take action?
Some argue that Yusuf was one of Farage’s original sugar-daddy donors, getting in there before the crypto kings and that he has a hold over Farage that puts him beyond discipline. I think it’s something else: Yusuf is very popular with the party’s membership. His framing - ‘we speak for the people, they’re all traitors’ - fits with how Reform’s most dedicated members see themselves. Lord Ashcroft is the only pollster to have gauged opinion on who members would most like to see succeed Farage, and found Yusuf clearly above Richard Tice and (a far less popular choice) Jenrick. Perhaps Yusuf is preparing for exactly this contest.
