====================================================================================================

Human rights groups sue Trump administration over ‘crippling’ ICC sanctions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:40:03 UTC
23:40:03 KST

Four prominent US human rights organizations have accused the Trump administration of using its “crippling” sanctions regime against the international criminal court (ICC) to undermine global efforts to prosecute war crimes and crimes against humanity.
In a lawsuit filed in federal court on Tuesday, the groups – the American Friends Service Committee, the Center for Constitutional Rights, Human Rights Watch and the Open Society Institute – argued that the administration’s targeting of the ICC, as well as of groups and individuals affiliated with it or assisting its work, amounts to a “blatantly illegal attack on international justice”, the groups said.
The suit challenges Trump’s February 2025 executive order, which authorized sanctions on ICC officials, judges and others working to support its investigation of Israeli crimes in Palestine – one of 18 investigations into war crimes and crimes against humanity the Netherlands-based tribunal is overseeing.
The US state department and justice department did not immediately respond to requests for comment.
The US government has long opposed and sought to impede the work of the court – of which it is not a party – but those attacks escalated after the ICC prosecutor issued arrest warrants in November 2024 for the Israeli prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, and former Israeli defense minister Yoav Gallant over crimes committed in Gaza. The US responded by sanctioning ICC prosecutors, judges, three leading Palestinian human rights groups and Francesca Albanese, the UN special rapporteur on the situation of human rights in Palestinian territory.
Earlier this summer, the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, also escalated efforts against the ICC, announcing a campaign aimed at “dismantling” it by pressuring member states to leave the court.
Those attacks, and Trump’s executive order, undermine access to justice for victims of major human rights violations and criminalize the work of those seeking to combat impunity by collaborating with the court – including the plaintiffs in the case – in violation of both constitutional protections for free speech and association and US obligations under international law, the lawsuit argues.
“The Trump administration took the extraordinary step to not only deny Palestinians and victims of US torture equal access to justice, but to criminalize and punish them, their lawyers and advocates, and their partners,” said Katherine Gallagher, a senior attorney at the Center for Constitutional Rights who represents victims before the ICC. “All victims of international crimes – from Sudan and Ukraine to Palestine and Afghanistan – need and deserve to have an independent and strong ICC able to fulfill its mission of ending impunity, without fear or favor.”
The individuals placed under US sanctions over their work for the ICC have been subjected to travel bans, have had their bank accounts closed or frozen, and access to digital services denied. Because of the dominance of US financial and technology institutions, the sanctions have trickled down and affected their ability to access services in other countries as well.
But the sanctions also affected rights groups not directly targeted – including the four plaintiffs – by hindering their ability to represent victims before the court, make legal and policy submissions to it, or collaborate with the sanctioned Palestinian groups on litigation, advocacy campaigns, research and humanitarian aid efforts.
“Because of the sanctions regime and the threat of severe criminal and civil penalties ... if I continued my work as a lawyer as before, I would have been or could be prosecuted or fined,” Gallagher said in a press conference held by the groups on Tuesday. “I have therefore ceased actively practicing before the ICC. [...] The government cannot dictate which clients lawyers can represent, which defendants are off limits from accountability, and what venue for justice and accountability are permissible.
Shawan Jabarin, the director of the human rights group Al Haq, one of the Palestinian organizations targeted by the Trump administration over its work with the ICC, told the Guardian in an interview last year that the sanctions had left the group unable to access its bank accounts or process donations, leaving about 45 people without pay.
But Jabarin also said the impact of the sanctions had extended beyond logistical matters and created a climate of fear by which longtime allies had cut communications with the group to avoid running afoul of the sanctions regime, with potential punishments of up to 20 years in prison and exorbitant fines.
“Most of them – if not all – they stopped working with us or engaging with us formally and openly,” Jabarin said at the time of several US groups with which Al Haq had collaborated in the past. He had called on those partners at the time to “fight back with all means”.
Tuesday’s lawsuit follows one filed last month by Democracy in the Arab World Now (Dawn), a Washington-based advocacy group focused on US foreign policy in the Middle East, and the New York-based Taxpayer Alliance Against Genocide, as well as legal challenges brought by some of the sanctioned individuals themselves, including Albanese’s family and three sitting ICC judges.
James Goldston, executive director of the Open Society Justice Initiative, a program of Open Society Foundations, which the Trump administration also explicitly threatened last year, called the sanctions “an assault on the rule of law, independent judges and prosecutors, and civil society in the United States and around the world”.
“Independent courts can function only when judges, prosecutors and rights advocates are free to do their work without fear of political retaliation. We believe that no one is above the law,” he said in the press conference on Tuesday. “The executive order shields the perpetrators of war crimes from scrutiny while punishing those who pursue accountability. Justice must be fair and impartial, not only directed at America’s adversaries.”

Assad ruled Syria with iron fist before defeat in civil war
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:39:35 UTC
23:39:35 KST

Assad ruled Syria with iron fist before defeat in civil war
DAMASCUS, Aug 11 - Bashar al-Assad's 24-year rule of Syria ended abruptly in December 2024 following a lightning advance by rebel forces who struck while the Russian and Iranian allies who had backed him through years of civil war were distracted by conflicts elsewhere.
He fled to Moscow, ending more than five decades of iron-fisted rule by his family and opening a new chapter for Syria.
The historic shift was underscored on Tuesday when a Damascus court sentenced him to death in his absence for crimes including killings, arbitrary arrests and torture during the years in which he clung to power.
Assad became president in 2000 after the death of his father, Hafez.
Shaped in its early years by the Iraq war and crisis in Lebanon, Assad's rule was defined by the civil war, which spiralled out of the 2011 Arab Spring, when Syrians demanding democracy took to the streets, to be met with deadly force.
Assad turned the full power of the Syrian military against his opponents as he sought to crush the rebellion inspired by the Arab Spring movement that had toppled leaders in Egypt and Tunisia.
Inquiries by the Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons and the United Nations concluded that government forces under Assad used the nerve agent sarin and chlorine as a weapon in attacks during the civil war on rebel-held areas.
Following one poison gas attack in 2018, then-U.S. President Donald Trump declared Assad an "animal". Authorities denied using chemical weapons, which killed and injured thousands during the war.
His ruthless approach to fighting rebels, including siege warfare tactics condemned as "medieval" by U.N. investigators, drew comparisons with the way his father had defeated Islamist insurgents in the 1980s.
Justifying his response, Assad compared himself to a surgeon cutting into a wound. "Do we say to him: 'your hands are covered in blood?' Or do we thank him for saving the patient?" he said in parliament in 2012.
Hundreds of thousands of people were killed in the war, cities were flattened, and more than a quarter of the population fled abroad.
A CRUMBLING ARMY
His grip on power appeared to be slipping in the first years of the war, as much of Syria fell to insurgents, some of them backed by Turkey, Gulf Arab states and the United States.
Shi'ite militias backed by his ally Iran began arriving in 2012 to fight on his side, shoring up his hold on Damascus. The arrival of Russia's air force in 2015 allowed Assad to go on the offensive and claw back the bulk of the country.
He outlasted many of the foreign leaders who believed his demise was imminent in the early days of the conflict.
But reliant on Moscow and Tehran for military support and with parts of Syria still beyond his grasp, Assad's rule remained precarious. In late 2024, insurgents seized a moment of opportunity, attacking when Iran had been weakened by conflict with Israel and Russia was distracted by its war in Ukraine.
Syria's demoralised army crumbled as rebels led by President Ahmed al-Sharaa's Islamist Hayat Tahrir al-Sham group swept towards Damascus. Assad left the capital, first to a Russian airbase in Latakia, before being evacuated to Moscow on December 8, 2024.
'STATE-RUN MACHINERY OF DEATH'
Prisoners freed from Syria's notorious jails recounted the torture and abuse they'd endured. An international war crimes prosecutor, visiting Syria after Assad was toppled, said evidence emerging from mass grave sites exposed a "state-run machinery of death". He accused the Assad-led state of having conducted organised killing comparable to Nazi Germany.
In October 2025, Reuters revealed that the Assad government had secretly moved thousands of bodies to a site in the desert east of Damascus, to hide evidence of atrocities.
From the start, Assad described the conflict as an international conspiracy against a country that had stood in opposition to the United States and its Middle Eastern allies, especially Israel. He characterised the rebels as foreign-backed Islamist extremists who would have been easily defeated were it not for their supporters overseas. He dismissed the idea that there was such a thing as a moderate Syrian opposition.
After Sharaa, a former al Qaeda commander, seized power in Damascus, Assad issued a written statement saying that "the state had fallen into the hands of terrorism".
BARREL BOMBS
Assad hailed from Syria's Alawite minority, a group which follows a religion that is an offshoot of Shi'ite Islam and had historically been based in Syria's coastal region.
Along with the presidency, he also inherited the leadership of the Syrian branch of the Baath Party, an Arab nationalist movement established in the 1940s.
Assad publicly held fast to the idea of Syria as a bastion of secular Arab nationalism even as the conflict appeared ever more sectarian, pitting his Alawite-led state and its Shi'ite backer Iran against insurgents drawn from the Sunni Muslim majority and supported by Sunni-led states.
The use of chemical weapons against rebel-held areas of Syria marked some of the most extensive uses of poison gas since World War One.
The deadliest such attack was on rebel-held Ghouta in 2013. The attack with sarin gas killed hundreds of people. It prompted no Western military response. The threat of a U.S. missile strike was averted when Moscow brokered a deal for Syria's chemical weapons to be destroyed by the following year.
After another sarin attack, in 2017, Trump ordered a cruise missile strike on a Syrian air base.
Earlier this year, the transitional leadership that took power after Assad was toppled said it had located remnants of a clandestine chemical weapons programme, including raw materials and munitions similar to those used to carry out deadly gas attacks during the civil war.
Assad consistently denied accusations that the state was to blame, in one instance accusing the global chemical weapons watchdog of fabricating a report about the use of chlorine gas at Washington's behest, echoing a broader pattern of rejecting allegations of state brutality.
In a BBC interview in 2015, he appeared to make light of accusations that the army was dropping barrel bombs – barrels packed with explosives that cause indiscriminate destruction – saying: "I haven't heard of the army using barrels, or maybe, cooking pots."
When tens of thousands of photos were smuggled out of Syria showing brutal torture of people in government custody, Assad dismissed it as part of a plot funded by Qatar.
In 2025, France issued an arrest warrant for Assad and other former Syrian officials over the bombing of a press center in the city of Homs in 2012.
EYE DOCTOR
Assad hadn't always been destined for the presidency: Hafez had groomed another son, Bassel, for the role. But when Bassel died in a car crash in 1994, Bashar was transformed from an eye doctor in London - where he studied as a postgraduate - to heir apparent.
The early days of his rule gave rise to cautious optimism about reform in Syria, where a feared security apparatus had underpinned Hafez al-Assad's rule since 1971. But this hope soon evaporated. The police state remained intact, perpetuating repression that ultimately fuelled the uprising against his rule in 2011.
Dissidents were jailed and economic reforms contributed to what U.S. diplomats described, in a 2008 embassy cable released by WikiLeaks, as "parasitic" nepotism and corruption.
While the elite did well, drought was driving the poor from rural areas to slums where the revolt would blaze.
He is married to British-born former investment banker, Asma Akhras, with whom he has three children. REUTERS

Air taxi company buys Boeing’s drone unit
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:38:45 UTC
23:38:45 KST

- Archer Aviation agreed to acquire Boeing subsidiaries Wisk Aero, Insitu and SkyGrid in an all-stock deal announced Monday, August 10, 2026.
- Boeing will receive Archer shares and warrants worth close to a 20 percent stake and commit up to $55 million to a future Archer funding round.
A pre-revenue air taxi startup has bought its way into the defense drone business, and Boeing is the one that sold it the keys.
Archer Aviation, the Silicon Valley company racing to put electric flying taxis over American cities, announced Monday that it has signed a definitive agreement to acquire three Boeing subsidiaries in a stock swap that instantly turns a company with no commercial revenue into the owner of a profitable military drone maker generating more than $200 million a year.
The deal, confirmed in a series of posts on X by Archer founder and CEO Adam Goldstein, hands Archer control of Wisk Aero, an autonomous flight developer that Boeing had bankrolled for years; Insitu, one of the world’s most established makers of intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance drones; and SkyGrid, a software company that builds digital air traffic management systems for uncrewed aircraft. In exchange, Boeing is not taking cash. It is taking equity, positioning itself to become one of Archer’s largest outside shareholders with a stake of nearly 20 percent, according to Archer’s own investor announcement of the transaction.
Archer’s stock jumped more than 20 percent in the trading session following the announcement, a reaction that reflects just how much weight investors put on the addition of Insitu’s balance sheet to a company that has spent years burning cash on aircraft certification with little revenue to show for it.
Insitu is the piece that changes Archer’s story overnight. Founded to help commercial fishing boats spot schools of tuna from the air, the company pivoted toward military reconnaissance in the early 2000s and built the ScanEagle, a small, catapult-launched surveillance drone that has logged more than 10,000 hours of real-world flight time since entering service in 2005. The ScanEagle carries a cruise speed of roughly 50 to 60 knots, a flight ceiling near 19,000 feet (5,800 meters) and an endurance of up to 18 hours, numbers that made it a workhorse for the U.S. Navy and Marine Corps and for allied militaries in places like Canada, Poland, Australia and Malaysia. Insitu later scaled that design up into the RQ-21A Blackjack, a larger tactical drone with a wingspan of 4.9 meters (15.7 feet) that replaced the Marine Corps’ older Shadow drones in 2018. Across its history, the company has built more than 3,500 uncrewed aircraft systems now operating with armed forces in 35 countries, a footprint that Goldstein’s own posts described as a key piece of Archer’s diversification strategy.
Wisk Aero brings a different kind of value. The company has spent roughly two decades developing self-flying aircraft, most recently flying its sixth-generation autonomous vehicle in December 2025 after more than 1,750 test flights, and it holds the distinction of being the only company to have designed, built and flown a piloted-optional aircraft with no pilot on board at all. SkyGrid rounds out the trio with airspace management technology it has developed since 2018, software designed to help regulators and operators track and coordinate large numbers of autonomous aircraft sharing the same skies, a problem that grows more urgent as drones and air taxis multiply over populated areas.
Archer plans to fold the software, flight histories and engineering knowledge from all three companies into ZEE, its in-house artificial intelligence platform built specifically for aerospace and defense applications. Goldstein framed the SkyGrid piece of that plan directly in his own words posted to X. “SkyGrid is highly complementary to ZEE as it has been developing next-generation airspace management solutions since 2018. That tech is in use today, and I believe SkyGrid plus ZEE can become a “must have” air traffic management product that helps modernize airspaces around the world. I see a clear path to significant AI revenue, and the team is heads-down building toward that.”
Archer announced last month, in partnership with defense technology firm Anduril Industries, a dual-use aircraft platform called Halo for commercial customers and Thunder for military ones, unveiled together at the Farnborough International Airshow in England in July 2026. The two variants share the same airframe, hybrid-electric powertrain and core flight systems, built to be produced quickly and cheaply using commercial supply chains rather than the slower, costlier processes typical of traditional defense manufacturing. Goldstein has described that platform as a massive opportunity spanning both commercial and defense markets, and the Boeing acquisition gives Archer the autonomy engineering talent and the existing defense customer relationships to try to make good on that bet at scale.
As part of the agreement, the aerospace giant secured a cross-licensing arrangement giving it continued access to Wisk’s core autonomous flight systems for use in its own current and future commercial and defense aircraft, along with a commitment to invest up to $55 million in an upcoming Archer funding round. The arrangement lets Boeing keep a hand in the autonomy race it helped fund for years while shedding the operational costs of running three separate subsidiaries that sat outside its core focus on commercial jets and defense programs.

====================================================================================================

Hawks, isolationists, and real estate developers: Inside the factions battling to shape U.S. foreign policy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:38:01 UTC
23:38:01 KST

At first glance, Donald Trump’s foreign policy appears to be a monolithic embodiment of the “America First” slogan. In reality, however, behind the MAGA façade lies a fierce bureaucratic struggle between several competing factions, each with its own vision of how the United States should project its power throughout the world. Analysts at the European Council on Foreign Relations (ECFR), for example, divide the foreign policy establishment surrounding Trump into radical isolationists (Restrainers), advocates of prioritizing the Indo-Pacific (Prioritizers), globalist hawks (Primacists), and cynical pragmatists from the business world (Developers). The categories are admittedly broad — and somewhat artificial — but the very fact that these disparate camps coexist within the administration helps explain the inconsistency and unpredictability of American policy under Trump.
Four major factions are commonly identified as leaders in the competition to shape U.S. foreign policy under Donald Trump. The first, representing the radical populist wing led by Vice President JD Vance (often seconded by Tucker Carlson), is fixated on rebuilding America at home. Its members call for an immediate end to aid for Ukraine, the freezing of ongoing conflicts, abandoning America’s informal role of global policeman, and imposing steep protectionist tariffs even on traditional allies (whom they openly regard as free riders).
The populists’ central argument — that “forever wars” are ruinous — is shared in part by the second faction, whose leading ideologue is former Pentagon official Elbridge Colby (backed by Senator Josh Hawley). These advocates of strict strategic prioritization argue that America's overstretched defense industrial base cannot sustain its current count of confrontations on multiple fronts and that Washington should thus redeploy its naval forces and advanced technologies to the Indo-Pacific for what they see as an existential struggle with China. Under this scenario, responsibility for Europe's security — and for Ukraine's future — would be shifted onto the Europeans themselves.
Both of these camps are fiercely opposed by the traditional Reaganite hawks, led by Secretary of State Marco Rubio (and his predecessor as National Security Advisor, Mike Waltz). They embrace the doctrine of “peace through strength,” arguing that an American retreat from Europe would only signal the kind of weakness that could encourage Beijing to invade Taiwan. Accordingly, they argue that the United States must maintain overwhelming military dominance across all key regions of the world in order to pressure the informal axis of Russia, China, and Iran, while simultaneously pushing NATO allies to increase their defense spending.
The fourth faction, made up of business-oriented investors led by Jared Kushner, seeks to reconcile the financial interests of the Saudi monarchy with a pro-Israel vision of the global order.
At the center of this political cacophony stands Trump himself. A transactional pragmatist, he has shown little hesitation in alternating between these mutually contradictory approaches (his critics like to joke that he invariably echoes the views of whichever adviser spoke to him last). He readily borrows Vance's isolationist rhetoric to mobilize his core electorate and shares Colby's fixation on the threat posed by China, while at the same time deliberately appointing uncompromising hawks to key positions. Critics describe this as foreign policy schizophrenia, whereas supporters argue that Trump has turned the chaos and unpredictability of his own personnel decisions into his greatest political asset.
Restrainers — the new isolationists
The isolationist “restrainers” previously appeared to be the chief intellectual force driving the MAGA movement, and the group's position had seemed unassailable after JD Vance was appointed vice president. However, the Trump administration's military campaigns against Iran exposed a clear fault line within the president’s inner circle, with the isolationists losing out in their confrontation with the Republican Party's traditional hawks — at least when it came to the Middle East.
In addition to Vice President Vance and his longtime media ally Tucker Carlson, this camp has traditionally included Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene and a collection of other hardline Trump loyalists in the House of Representatives. Its intellectual backbone consists of organizations such as the Quincy Institute for Responsible Statecraft and the right wing of the Heritage Foundation, which has sought to provide a theoretical foundation for American neo-isolationism.
The faction's electoral base is made up of the disillusioned working class of America's Rust Belt who most eagerly consume content from the movement’s sprawling network of alternative media outlets and far-right activists. Weary of “forever wars,” this faction has even found common cause with left-wing anti-imperialists (like Tulsi Gabbard).
The isolationists' immediate objectives have always been highly practical. They seek to block all financial and military aid to Kyiv, force America's European NATO allies to pay for their own security, and redirect the resulting savings toward securing the southern border. Over the longer term, however, the group aims to dismantle the global security architecture created after World War II, replacing it with rigid economic protectionism and sweeping tariffs. Their ideal is to turn the United States into a self-sufficient “fortress” that attacks foreign markets with tariffs rather than missiles. Achieving that vision would require the complete abandonment of the Republican party’s Reagan-era legacy of internationalism.
The isolationists' ideal is to turn the United States into a self-sufficient “fortress,” attacking foreign markets with tariffs rather than missiles
In their struggle for influence, the restrainers work closely with right-wing populist leaders in Europe, above all former Hungarian Prime Minister Viktor Orbán. They also rely on the backing of tech billionaires led by Elon Musk, who shares the group’s skepticism toward Washington's global commitments.
Their principal adversaries are the traditional Washington establishment, the defense-industrial complex, and the powerful pro-Israel lobby represented by the Republican Jewish Coalition. Within the administration itself, they have been engaged in an unrelenting bureaucratic struggle with the globalist hawks led by Rubio and Waltz — who ultimately seized the initiative by drawing the White House into a major military escalation in the Middle East.
Recent events have led to a de facto split within the isolationist camp itself. Tucker Carlson, who publicly denounced the strikes on Iran as a vile and immoral decision driven by Benjamin Netanyahu's manipulation, has definitively broken with Trump, saying he deeply regrets his past support for the president. Like Marjorie Taylor Greene, his dramatic split launched a public debate over the potential creation of an independent political movement.
At the same time, JD Vance has been forced to play an extremely careful game of internal politics, balancing loyalty to his boss against his own presidential ambitions for 2028. In his public appearances, the vice president has had to defend Trump's hard-line policy on Iran while assuring the press that he does not take orders from Carlson.
Trump himself, driven solely by transactional calculations and a desire for quick geopolitical victories, has for now pushed the restrainers to the sidelines. Responding to Carlson's criticism, he remarked that the real MAGA movement supports the president’s decisive actions — making it clear that the isolationist agenda has lost at least some of its influence to Washington’s familiar advocates of military force.
Prioritizers — strategic prioritizers with an anti-China focus
The faction of prioritizers, which has become the intellectual backbone of the Defense Department during Donald Trump's second term, represents the most pragmatic — and, for America's European allies, the most troubling — wing of the revamped Republican foreign policy establishment.
Unlike the outright isolationists, who dream of retreating behind America's borders, this camp thinks in terms of geopolitical “realism.” Its leading figures include Elbridge Colby (the grandson of a former CIA director), who serves as the Pentagon's under secretary of defense for policy, and populist Senator Josh Hawley. The group's ideological core also includes Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, under whose nominal leadership Colby has effectively taken the lead in shaping America's new National Defense Strategy. Other prominent members include former Rep. Mike Gallagher, along with analysts at the Marathon Initiative, a think tank co-founded by Colby.
The faction's principal financial backers come from Silicon Valley's conservative wing, most notably venture capitalists such as Peter Thiel and Palmer Luckey, (founder of the defense startup Anduril). They are interested in redirecting government contracts toward artificial intelligence, satellite reconnaissance, and autonomous combat systems capable of competing with China.
The prioritizers' immediate tactical objectives involve suspending the deployment of purportedly scarce U.S. resources currently going to Ukraine and Europe and directing them towards Asia instead. In the short term, they seek to freeze all secondary conflicts in order to rapidly redeploy American aircraft carriers, air defense systems, and precision-guided missile capabilities to the Taiwan Strait.
Their long-term strategic objective is to prevent Beijing from establishing hegemony in Asia and to carry out a sweeping economic and technological decoupling that would completely cut China off from Western intellectual and financial resources. To that end, Hawley has introduced legislation in the Senate calling for a radical separation of American and Chinese artificial intelligence capabilities, banning all U.S. investment in China, and imposing severe penalties on American corporations that transfer technology there. In the prioritizers’ worldview, the European Union is seen not as a historic ally bound to the United States by shared values, but as a geopolitical free rider and potential economic competitor that must be forced to achieve full strategic autonomy, even if doing so leads to the collapse of transatlantic unity.
The prioritizers' long-term strategic objective is to prevent Beijing from achieving hegemony in Asia
On the international stage, the prioritizers are working to develop an architecture of bilateral partnerships, working closely with Australia through the AUKUS alliance, as well as with Japan and Taiwan. Colby has demanded unequivocal commitments from these partners to commit substantial resources in the event of a conflict with China — including by engaging in the fight directly.
At the same time, the prioritizers are engaged in two relentless bureaucratic struggles back in Washington — defending themselves against the globalist hawks led by Rubio, and supporting Congressional efforts to investigate major technology companies such as Meta over senior executives’ secret concessions to the Chinese Communist Party, made in order to preserve their access to Asian markets.
Colby and his team are now fully focused on implementing a new doctrine of flexible realism. They have been traveling to regional summits, where they have sought to build barriers against China's expanding influence by forcing America's allies to choose sides.
The group has retained Trump's favor because it provides the president with an ideal intellectual justification for his distrust of European leaders. At the same time, however, its narrow strategic focus and reluctance to divert attention to other regions periodically irritate the White House — especially when Trump becomes captivated by dramatic escalations in the Middle East that call for a classic display of imperial power rather than the long-term planning required for a decades-long technological competition with China.
Primacists — Washington's global hawks
The primacist faction, or global hawks, represents the Reaganite neoconservative wing of the Republican Party. Despite fierce resistance from the isolationists, this group has staged an impressive bureaucratic comeback during Donald Trump's second term. Its leading figures include Rubio and Waltz, along with former National Security Advisor Robert O'Brien, former Secretary of State Mike Pompeo, and influential Republicans on key congressional committees, including Michael McCaul and Mike Turner.
The faction's intellectual and financial foundation rests on the traditional American defense-industrial complex (represented by giants such as Lockheed Martin and Raytheon), the old guard of the intelligence community, and the influential pro-Israel lobby (including the Republican Jewish Coalition). They see the primacists as the last line of defense against a complete American withdrawal from the world's key geopolitical flashpoints.
The global hawks' immediate tactical objectives center on projecting overwhelming military power and containing Washington's principal adversaries through direct escalation. This was clearly reflected in their role as the leading advocates of the military campaign against Iran and the large-scale strikes in Venezuela in early 2026.
Over the long term, the primacists seek to preserve total U.S. dominance across all three critical regions — Europe, Asia, and the Middle East — and they believe any concession to Russia or Iran would immediately encourage China to move against Taiwan. To that end, they are reshaping America's traditional alliances, promoting concepts such as the “Shield of the Americas” while demanding that European NATO members assume full operational responsibility for the continent's security while continuing to act under American leadership.
The hawks seek to preserve total U.S. dominance across all three critical regions: Europe, Asia, and the Middle East
On the international stage, the primacists closely coordinate with Benjamin Netanyahu in Israel and with right-wing Latin American regimes while simultaneously waging an intense behind-the-scenes struggle in Washington. Their principal rivals remain JD Vance’s restrainers (who accuse the hawks of dragging the United States into endless proxy wars) and Elbridge Colby's camp of Asia-focused pragmatists (who argue that Washington should abandon Europe in order to concentrate on Taiwan).
Rubio's team is currently consumed with managing the aftermath of the strikes on Iran, shaping a new security architecture in South America, and protecting the Pentagon’s bureaucracy from the influence of the prioritizers. The global hawks enjoy Trump's favor only so long as their operations deliver quick, headline-grabbing victories that benefit the president politically — like the capture of Venezuelan dictator Nicolas Maduro in January. Their influence nevertheless remains precarious, as Trump could pivot back toward isolationism if protracted military campaigns begin to damage his domestic approval ratings.
The sudden death of Sen. Lindsey Graham on July 11 — just days after his tenth visit to Kyiv, came as a profound shock to the global hawks. Graham consistently championed the neoconservative doctrine of "peace through strength,” steadfastly supported Ukraine, advocated for maximum pressure on Iran, and regarded NATO as one of America's most important strategic assets, firmly rejecting calls to scale back the country's global presence. Graham's unique strength lay in his remarkable ability to translate geopolitical strategy into the “language of deals” that Trump understood best. For example, he successfully lobbied for continued aid to Kyiv by pragmatically emphasizing Ukraine's lithium and titanium reserves, arguing that they could not be allowed to fall into the hands of Moscow or Beijing. Through countless rounds of "golf diplomacy," the senator effectively implanted traditional hawkish ideas directly into the president's thinking, skillfully counterbalancing the influence of Vance's radical isolationists.
Shortly before his death, Graham had even secured White House approval for a new package of sanctions against Russia. His passing, however, has created a critical vacuum: deprived of their most effective political negotiator, the hawks now risk ceding control of U.S. foreign policy to the isolationists.
Developers — New York dealmakers
The faction of transactional business pragmatists — the so-called “developer diplomacy” team led by Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner — has emerged as the most secretive and financially driven center of influence in U.S. foreign policy. Kushner's official appointment in February 2026 to the newly created position of special envoy for peace formalized what had previously been an informal network. In addition to Kushner, the group includes his closest partner and ally, New York real estate magnate Steve Witkoff, who simultaneously serves as special envoy for the Middle East and as the administration's chief negotiator with the Kremlin.
The team is backed by the sovereign wealth funds of the Gulf states — above all Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund. It also enjoys financial support from the ruling elites of the United Arab Emirates and Qatar, which view Kushner and Witkoff as reliable interlocutors capable of converting American geopolitical influence into concrete investment projects.
The developers' immediate tactical objectives are centered on concluding the highly sensitive backchannel negotiations between the United States and Iran. Working through intermediaries in Doha and Oman, Kushner and Witkoff are seeking to finalize the terms of a nuclear agreement while launching large-scale postwar reconstruction projects in the Gaza Strip that would transform its ruins into potentially lucrative waterfront real estate. The group's long-term strategy is to replace traditional alliances and international institutions with a network of cross-border commercial consortia, in which regional stability would be guaranteed not by an American military presence but by the intertwining of capital from authoritarian regimes and Western investors.
The group's long-term strategy is to replace traditional alliances and international institutions with a network of cross-border commercial consortia
On the international stage, they work closely with the Gulf monarchies and Israel's right-wing government. At the same time, they are engaged in an intense behind-the-scenes struggle against Rubio’s global hawks, who view Kushner's business-oriented approach as a threat to U.S. national security, as the developers’ approach exhibits a willingness to accommodate authoritarian regimes in pursuit of personal gain.
In Washington, Kushner's team is under a barrage of investigations by congressional Democrats and watchdog organizations such as CREW, which accuse the envoys of unprecedented conflicts of interest. By mid-2026, the assets under the management of Kushner's investment firm, Affinity Partners, had surpassed $6 billion — much of it coming from foreign governments with which Kushner was simultaneously conducting official negotiations on behalf of the White House. At the same time, Witkoff has been accused of promoting the cryptocurrency venture World Liberty Financial (launched together with Trump's sons) to Middle Eastern sovereign investors.
Kushner and Witkoff are currently preoccupied with preserving the fragile June 2026 ceasefire with Tehran in order to prevent repeated regional exchanges of fire from derailing the technical consultations taking place in Qatar. Despite mounting criticism and legal scrutiny, Kushner remains under Trump's protection. His ability to package geopolitical crises into business proposals that resonate with the president — combined with the administration's disregard for the bureaucratic conventions of old Washington — has been enough to turn him into Trump’s personal envoy.
At the same time, however, the lack of meaningful diplomatic experience on the Kushner-Witkoff team has undermined their initiatives. That has been particularly evident in Moscow, where transactional business deals have proven ineffective against the Kremlin's uncompromising imperial ambitions.
The battle of Washington's power centers
By the middle of 2026, the internal struggle over foreign policy within the Trump administration had escalated into an all-out battle for survival. Each of the four factions has sought to secure its own independent sources of power, reducing its dependence on the president's shifting preferences. The primacists (global hawks) led by Marco Rubio and Robert O'Brien possess the deepest institutional roots and the strongest external backing. They are supported by the traditional American defense-industrial complex, the Republican Party's old establishment, and powerful lobbying organizations such as AIPAC.
The prioritizers (Asia-focused hawks) led by Elbridge Colby and Josh Hawley have built a very different power base. They rely on Silicon Valley's defense-tech ecosystem and billionaires such as Peter Thiel and Palmer Luckey, who finance startups developing autonomous drones and artificial intelligence systems for the Pentagon.
The restrainers (isolationists) led by JD Vance draw their strength directly from the right-wing populist media empires of Elon Musk and Tucker Carlson, giving them direct access to the MAGA grassroots.
And the developers (business world “pragmatists”) led by Jared Kushner stand out as a genuinely transnational force, with support coming not from American institutions but from the multibillion-dollar sovereign wealth funds of Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates.
This diversification of political and financial resources directly shapes the standing of each faction's leaders among ordinary Americans as the country approaches the 2026 midterm elections and the 2028 presidential race.
Marco Rubio remains the most popular figure among moderate conservatives and independent voters, embodying a sense of stability and traditional American strength. By contrast, JD Vance, despite serving as vice president and being regarded as Trump's likely successor (at least by the party's more radical wing), remains a deeply polarizing figure with very high unfavorability ratings among centrists. His ally Tucker Carlson, meanwhile, commands enormous but highly polarizing popularity, making him more of a media kingmaker than a plausible electoral candidate.
The prioritizers' intellectual leader, Elbridge Colby, is virtually unknown to the broader public. He deliberately avoids electoral politics, preferring to work behind the scenes at the Department of Defense. Jared Kushner, meanwhile, consistently ranks among America's least popular and least trusted political figures due to allegations of nepotism and his cynical blending of public office with private business interests.
The bureaucratic and ideological confrontation among these factions has intensified with each passing month as the battle to inherit Trump's political legacy in 2028 rages on. Vance is attempting to use the vice presidency to purge the party apparatus of the Republican old guard. His position, however, has been significantly weakened by Tucker Carlson's open break with the administration.
Rubio and O'Brien quickly moved to exploit that split. Through loyal allies on congressional committees, they are seeking to convince Trump that only their vision of global American primacy can guarantee the Republican party victories at the ballot box.
Colby's prioritizers are pursuing a more subtle strategy, trying to establish confrontation with China as a permanent pillar of U.S. national security doctrine — one that would outlast any future president. Kushner, meanwhile, has largely distanced himself from electoral politics, focusing instead on locking in his Middle Eastern business deals before Trump leaves the Oval Office.
On the question of Israel, Rubio's primacists and Kushner's business diplomats alike advocate unwavering support for Jerusalem. Kushner, in particular, views a firmly pro-Israel policy as the foundation for bringing Saudi Arabia into his broader economic projects. The prioritizers, by contrast, are considerably less enthusiastic about supporting Israel, arguing that Washington should remain focused on Taiwan. The isolationist wing surrounding Carlson has gone even further, launching sharp attacks on Benjamin Netanyahu that accuse the Israeli prime minister of manipulating the U.S. military in order to drag America into a major Middle Eastern war.
The differences between the factions are also evident in their responses to less visible crises. One example of this behind-the-scenes struggle has been the debate over Trump's repeated threats to launch direct military or missile strikes against Mexican drug cartels on Mexican territory. This hard-line proposal has been enthusiastically embraced by the isolationists around JD Vance, who see it as the literal embodiment of the promise to defend America's borders. They are joined by Rubio’s global hawks, who view military action in Latin America as a classic demonstration of American power inside Washington's geopolitical backyard. Colby's Asia-focused hawks, however, have expressed frustration with such plans, arguing that drawing the Pentagon into a prolonged and resource-intensive counterterrorism campaign on America's doorstep would tie up scarce intelligence assets, special operations forces, and logistical capabilities that are critically needed to deter China in the Taiwan Strait.
An equally fierce conceptual clash is unfolding over technological protectionism and the regulation of artificial intelligence. In this arena, Colby's prioritizers, backed by Silicon Valley defense technology advocates, argue that American defense-tech startups should be granted maximum regulatory freedom while cutting China off from access to advanced semiconductors in order to win the next-generation global arms race. This military-technological approach is directly opposed by Jared Kushner's camp, which views artificial intelligence not as a doomsday weapon but as an unprecedented commercial opportunity. The group is currently working to channel billions of dollars from the sovereign wealth funds of Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates into the construction of massive data centers. Such commercial opportunism on the part of the president's son-in-law has infuriated national security hawks of every stripe, who accuse Kushner of being willing to place the critical infrastructure of the future under the de facto control of authoritarian Gulf monarchies for the sake of personal financial gain.
In the long run, experts generally regard Colby's prioritizers as the faction with the greatest staying power. Their narrow focus on the technological competition with China aligns closely with the broad consensus among America's traditional conservatives and the intelligence community.
Rubio's global hawks, for their part, are widely regarded as highly effective bureaucratic operators, capable of skillfully managing the machinery of government. Their vision of the United States as the global policeman, however, is increasingly viewed as outdated and is rapidly losing support within a changing Republican Party.
Kushner's faction, by contrast, is generally seen as having only short-term, opportunistic potential — capable of delivering high-profile but fragile diplomatic successes that could quickly unravel in the face of any major systemic crisis.
Experts assign the lowest marks for governing capacity to the isolationists led by Vance and Carlson. Their overwhelming dependence on Trump's personal favor, along with their internal divisions and lack of experienced personnel to fill senior government positions, leave them highly vulnerable to the institutional weight of Washington's traditional bureaucracy, which has repeatedly managed to reassert control over the actual conduct of White House policy.

France’s oldest lumberjack contest puts axe skills to the test
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:37:53 UTC
23:37:53 KST

Around 60 competitors gathered in Chamrousse on Sunday 9 August for France’s 73rd competitive lumberjacking championship, racing to cut through logs by hand with axes in a test of speed, accuracy and endurance. Timekeepers stopped the clock as each section finally broke free, with the fastest run of the day recorded at 4.49 seconds.
The championship has been held at the Alpine resort south-east of Grenoble since 1953, preserving a tradition closely tied to the region’s forestry history. Between rounds, competitors prepare identical logs and sharpen their axes, while some wear chainmail guards to protect their legs from accidental blows.
Eight-time French champion Pierre Puybaret, who also holds the world record in single-buck sawing, was among the competitors. Organiser Pascal Gaidet said the event started as a friendly contest between local lumberjacks before growing to attract participants from neighbouring regions.
With recent editions drawing around 2,000 spectators, the competition keeps a traditional logging skill alive even as chainsaws and mechanical harvesters have transformed modern forestry.

European football returns as continent’s top leagues prepare for new season
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:37:26 UTC
23:37:26 KST

Halis Sünnetci
11 August 2026•Update: 11 August 2026
- Barcelona, Arsenal, Inter Milan, PSG and Bayern Munich prepare to defend domestic titles
European club football is set to return following a summer dominated by the 2026 FIFA World Cup, with Spain's La Liga getting the new season underway this weekend before the continent's other major domestic leagues follow later in August.
The 2026-27 campaign comes after an unusually compressed summer, with the World Cup in the US, Canada and Mexico coming to a close on July 19 and leaving many international players with only a short break before returning to their clubs.
Spain will be the first of Europe's "big five" leagues to get underway, with the new La Liga season beginning on the weekend of Aug. 14-16.
The Spanish title race will again put the spotlight on heavyweights Barcelona and Real Madrid, while Atletico Madrid will seek to challenge the country's traditional top two.
The first El Clasico of the campaign is scheduled for Oct. 25, when Barcelona host Real Madrid, with the return fixture in Madrid set for May 9.
Premier League begins Aug. 21
England's Premier League will return a week later, with defending champions Arsenal hosting newly promoted Coventry City at the Emirates Stadium in the opening match on Friday, Aug. 21.
Manchester City will begin their campaign against Bournemouth, while the 38-match season is scheduled to conclude on May 30, 2027.
The new campaign will see Arsenal attempt to defend their crown against a group of challengers traditionally led by Manchester City, Liverpool, Chelsea and Manchester United.
Coventry City will be among the newcomers to the division, adding another chapter to the club's return to the top level of English football.
Inter begin Serie A title defense
Italy's Serie A will kick off on the weekend of Aug. 22-23 and run through May 30.
Defending champions Inter Milan will open their campaign at home against promoted Monza on Aug. 22.
The Milan club will enter the season seeking to retain the Scudetto amid competition from rivals including Napoli, Juventus and AC Milan.
Serie A's calendar will feature two midweek rounds during a season shaped by both domestic and European commitments.
PSG remain team to beat in France
France's Ligue 1 will also return on the weekend of Aug. 21-23.
Paris Saint-Germain will once again begin as the team to beat in France, while Marseille, Monaco and other challengers will look to close the gap.
PSG went into training in late July following the World Cup, facing a particularly demanding August schedule as they return to domestic competition.
The Ligue 1 season is scheduled to conclude on May 29, 2027.
Bundesliga starts latest after World Cup
Germany will be the last of Europe's five biggest leagues to begin its season.
The Bundesliga will kick off on Aug. 28, one week later than last season because of the World Cup.
Defending champions Bayern Munich will host VfB Stuttgart at the Allianz Arena in the opening fixture.
Bayern secured their second consecutive Bundesliga title last season, sealing the championship with a 4-2 victory over Stuttgart in April, adding an extra dimension to the opening game.
Most Bundesliga clubs will nevertheless return to competitive action one week earlier, with the first round of the German Cup scheduled for Aug. 21-24.
The Bundesliga season will conclude on May 22, 2027.
New European season follows World Cup summer
The staggered starts mean Europe's major domestic leagues will gradually return over a two-week period, beginning with La Liga this weekend and culminating with the Bundesliga at the end of August.
The new campaign will also quickly turn attention toward European competition, with the UEFA Champions League league phase beginning in September.
For many of Europe's leading clubs, the central challenge will be managing players returning from an extended international summer while navigating increasingly crowded domestic and European schedules.

Amsterdam transport chief hid €850m bridge costs before election
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:36:22 UTC
23:36:22 KST

Amsterdam transport chief hid €850m bridge costs before election
See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results
Amsterdam’s former transport chief, Melanie van der Horst, knew before March’s local elections that a planned cycle and pedestrian bridge over the IJ would cost up to €850 million – more than double the sum set aside – but did not tell the city council, documents released under a freedom of information request show.
Van der Horst, who is now the city’s head of economic affairs and was also lead candidate for the social-liberal party D66 at the elections, had been told by officials that the estimate for the Oostbrug had risen to between €650 million and €850 million, compared with the €325 million budgeted. The papers were obtained by Amsterdam broadcaster AT5.
Officials circulated the final estimate internally around December 2 and, according to AT5, explicitly gave Van der Horst the choice of making the figures public before or after the vote. At a meeting on February 12 it was decided to keep the older, lower amounts in the city’s official investment note.
Eight days before the elections Van der Horst was given a presentation setting out how the new range had been calculated. During the campaign she cited the IJ crossings as one of her administration’s achievements.
The new estimates only surfaced in early June, when the city published the documents handed to coalition negotiators.
The bridge, which at 1.2 kilometres would be one of the longest in the country, is being paid for jointly by the council and the regional transport authority Vervoerregio Amsterdam.
Emergency debate
 Seven opposition parties – PvdD, Volt, JA21, Denk, BIJ1, CDA and SP – said in a joint statement they were shocked by the revelations but were not yet drawing conclusions, and an emergency debate has been requested. The coalition party PRO and D66’s own council group have also said they want answers.
Van der Horst declined to comment, referring questions to Elise Moeskops (D66), who took over the transport brief. Moeskops said the provisional estimates carry wide margins and could still change, that work on the design is continuing and that a final figure is not expected until early next year.

No 200,000 votes? Drop out: Israel’s small parties face a much higher election threshold
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:35:18 UTC
23:35:18 KST

With 77 days remaining until Israel’s election, the political blocs are still taking shape and the final party lineup remains far from settled. One factor, however, is becoming increasingly important: small parties hovering around the electoral threshold could ultimately determine who forms the next government.
A new analysis published Tuesday by the Taub Center for Social Policy Studies in Israel warns that the number of votes required to enter the Knesset is expected to rise sharply, largely because Israel’s eligible voting population has grown substantially since the 2022 election.
If the scenarios outlined by the center materialize, and no major mergers take place among smaller parties, several could struggle to clear the electoral threshold, potentially leaving hundreds of thousands of votes unrepresented.
In the 2022 election, a party needed roughly 155,000 valid votes to cross the threshold. Nearly 420,000 votes, or 8.8% of all ballots cast, ultimately went to parties that failed to enter the Knesset, an amount equivalent to roughly 10 parliamentary seats.
This time, the bar is expected to be considerably higher.
The number of Israelis eligible to vote has risen from 6.789 million in November 2022 to an estimated 7.561 million by the end of October 2026. The increase stems both from large age cohorts reaching voting age and from significant waves of new Israeli citizens receiving citizenship.
How many votes will a party need in the 2026 election? The answer depends on both the size of the electorate and turnout.
According to the Taub Center’s scenarios, if turnout reaches 70%, similar to the previous election, a party would need around 171,000 valid votes to clear the threshold.
At 75% turnout, the required number would rise to roughly 183,000.
And if turnout reaches 80%, slightly above levels recorded in Israel from the 1970s through the 1990s, a party would need approximately 195,000 votes to enter the Knesset.
Even a threshold of 160,000 votes would pose a significant obstacle for smaller parties. That could affect parties or lists associated with Chili Tropper and Yoaz Hendel, Benny Gantz, Gilad Erdan and Yuli Edelstein, and potentially Ofer Winter, leaving several at risk of falling short.
Political officials believe Tropper, who has previously said he would not allow votes to be wasted, would seek a merger if polls close to the deadline for submitting candidate lists showed his party failing to clear the threshold safely.
Regarding Erdan and Edelstein, political assessments suggest that even if their parties fail to enter the Knesset, the votes lost could be divided relatively evenly between Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s bloc and the opposing bloc.
Prof. Alex Weinreb, who authored the Taub Center paper, said every increase in turnout would push the numerical threshold higher. According to his calculations, each one-percentage-point increase in voter turnout raises the number of votes required to enter the Knesset by approximately 2,240.
The implication, he said, is that leaders of parties unable to secure support from at least 200,000 voters may be directly endangering their political bloc.
Large numbers of voters ending up without parliamentary representation, he added, could also damage public confidence in democracy and state institutions.
“Polls give an indication of the level of support for each party relative to the electoral threshold, but it is important not to confuse the percentage threshold with the actual number of votes needed to cross it. The latter determines who enters the Knesset,” Weinreb said.
“It is important that in the coming weeks, leaders of small parties and their advisers keep this in mind when they look at the numbers. They need to remember that even the most reliable polls have a margin of error around every estimate, and that margin also grows when percentages are converted into absolute numbers.
“Given the rapid growth in Israel’s voting-age population and expectations of particularly high turnout in the coming election, my message to those people is simple: If your party does not have 200,000 votes, do the right thing and leave the race. Find another way to contribute to the country.”
The Taub Center for Social Policy Studies in Israel is an independent, nonpartisan research institute focusing on economic and social policy. It provides policymakers and the public with research and data on issues including education, health care, welfare, the labor market and economic policy.

Hungary parliament elects former supreme court head Andras Baka as president
BUDAPEST, Aug 11 - Hungary's parliament elected Andras Baka, a former head of the Supreme Court, as the country's next president on Tuesday, a symbolic step in Prime Minister Peter Magyar's effort to dismantle former premier Viktor Orban's bastions of power.
Baka was elected to the largely ceremonial role with the votes of the governing centre-right Tisza party as members of the opposition Fidesz party did not take part in the election.
In a speech after taking the oath of office, Baka criticised the previous government, saying that during Orban's rule, "one party captured the state", branches of power were not separate and there was no functioning system of checks and balances.
However, he said that the task of building a new future for Hungary "demands both justice and self-restraint" and said that the president's role was to represent citizens who hold differing views.
"There is one thing we certainly cannot do, we cannot build the new Hungary on revenge," Baka said.
Magyar's election victory in April ended Orban's 16-year rule. He has vowed to restore democratic standards and called on key officials appointed or elected by Orban's party to resign.
Removing former President Tamas Sulyok was a key goal of Magyar, who called him a "puppet" of Orban. Sulyok last month signed a constitutional amendment passed by Tisza that ended his own presidency.
Under that amendment, the new president will serve until a new constitution takes effect, or for a maximum of five years.
Members of parliament from the opposition Fidesz party did not take part in the vote as they said in a statement earlier in the day that Tisza "arbitrarily and illegitimately terminated" Sulyok's term and accused the governing party of "tyranny."
Fidesz had accused Magyar's government of authoritarianism, which Tisza denied. REUTERS

====================================================================================================

Disrupted monsoons force India to face climate threat
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:35:00 UTC
23:35:00 KST

Disrupted monsoons force India to face climate threat
August 11, 2026
India's southwest monsoon usually arrives around June, brings most of the country's annual rainfall and begins to retreat by September.
Its timing and intensity have always varied, but the broad pattern was predictable enough for farmers, cities and businesses to plan around it. Now, this natural rhythm increasingly seems like a thing of the past.
More than 100 people have been killed across India since late July as heavy monsoon rains triggered floods, landslides and lightning strikes.
The northeastern state of Assam has borne the brunt, with more than 120,000 people affected by flooding. Kerala, Jammu and Kashmir, Bihar and Jharkhand have also reported deaths and widespread disruption.
The problem is not necessarily that India is receiving more rain. It is the way the rain is arriving: longer dry spells are followed by short bursts of intense rainfall that can overwhelm rivers, drainage systems and hillsides.
El Nino has added to this year's variability but scientists say the longer-term changes are being driven by a changing climate.
Have the monsoons entered a new era?
Akshay Deoras, a senior research scientist with the UK's National Centre for Atmospheric Science and the University of Reading, cautions against describing the Indian monsoon as having entered "a fundamentally different regime."
"Active and break spells have always been part of the monsoon. What appears to be changing is their intensity," Deoras told DW.
He pointed out a warmer atmosphere can hold more moisture, making active spells, or periods of intense rainfall, more intense.
Madhavan Nair Rajeevan, a climate scientist and former secretary in the Ministry of Earth Sciences, says the change is visible in the behaviour of the monsoon itself.
Average seasonal rainfall, he said, has not shown a systematic long-term change and the monsoon remains a robust natural phenomenon. But the timing, intensity and distribution of rainfall are all noticeably different.
"The characteristics of the Indian monsoon have changed fundamentally," Rajeevan told DW. "We are witnessing an increase in short-duration rainfall extremes, longer dry spells, and changes in the frequency, intensity and distribution of monsoon weather systems."
He believes the rise in extreme rainfall is more than natural variability.
"It represents a structural shift in the monsoon system," he said.
Villages and cities both at risk
India's farmers depend heavily on the timing of rainfall. Long dry spells can disrupt sowing and reduce soil moisture and intense rain can wash away fields and damage crops.
India's cities are also struggling due to rapid construction, as new roads and buildings leave less land to absorb water, thus interfering with natural drainage.
Moreover, after weeks of dry weather, hard, dry soil absorbs less water. When heavy rain finally arrives, more of it floods the rivers and drains instead of replenishing groundwater.
India not stepping up to extreme weather hazard
"The greatest risks increasingly come from short periods of very intense rainfall," Deoras told DW, warning that India's infrastructure is increasingly out of step with this changing threat.
Deoras says drainage systems and flood management are still too often designed around historical seasonal rainfall averages rather than the short, high-impact events that cause the greatest disruption.
He noted that forecasting has improved, but knowing that extreme rain is coming is only useful if authorities act on the warning.
Warnings, he said, need to translate into "timely, impact-based decisions" such as changing school schedules, restricting travel or closing off vulnerable areas before heavy rain arrives.
Ritwick Dutta, an environmental lawyer and managing trustee of the Legal Initiative for Forest and Environment, says the problem goes beyond extreme weather itself.
"It is now scientifically accepted that climate change leads to erratic rainfall patterns. High rainfall over a few days followed by long dry periods during the monsoon," Dutta told DW.
"These trends have been compounded by the fact that the decision-making process in India fails to consider climate change while planning construction projects."
Dutta points to the western city of Pune, where a study by the state government and the Energy and Resources Institute projects a 37% increase in rainfall by 2030. Still, Dutta says this projection has not been adequately incorporated into the city's planning, including its riverfront development.
"Dams, embankments and roads are all likely to suffer major damage due to climate change, and yet we proceed as though climate change is a matter to be discussed only in conferences and workshops," Dutta said.
"We are, in effect, 'weaponizing the environment'," Dutta said.
Dutta says India's environmental clearance system does not require climate change to be systematically factored into project decisions.
"The concept of 'climate proofing' does not exist here," he said.
Thousands killed in last year's monsoon
The Centre for Science and Environment (CSE) found that extreme weather affected India on 331 of 334 monitored days in 2025, or 99% of the period assessed.
More than 4,000 people died, about 3,000 during the monsoon, while at least 30 states and union territories experienced extreme weather simultaneously for eight consecutive months.
Sunita Narain, director general of the CSE, argues that adaptation therefore needs to become part of the country's development strategy.
"Climate change is no longer a future threat. It is already here in the form of extreme heat, extreme rainfall and increasingly destructive weather events," Narain told DW.
"The challenge is no longer proving the science, but redesigning our economy and infrastructure to survive it," she added.
"Cities, roads, drainage systems and water infrastructure can no longer be designed for yesterday's climate."
Every investment, she says, must be planned for a future of more intense and unpredictable weather.
In her reckoning, India must stop treating each flood as an isolated disaster and start planning for a climate in which such extremes are no longer exceptional.
What can be done against extreme weather?
India is facing a whole new pattern of extreme weather. It can record a rainfall deficit nationally while individual regions are underwater. Farmers can go weeks without useful rain and then lose crops in a few hours. Cities can prepare for an average monsoon and still be overwhelmed by one extraordinary downpour.
"The focus now needs to move beyond documenting and discussing these changes towards developing and implementing effective, sector-specific adaptation strategies," climate scientist and former Indian official Rajeevan said.
That means better flood management in cities, changes in agricultural practices, stronger water-storage systems and infrastructure designed around short-duration extreme rainfall rather than historical averages.
India cannot control the monsoon or El Nino and it cannot stop global warming on its own. It can decide, however, where it builds, how it builds and if its new infrastructure is designed for the climate of its future.
Edited by: Darko Janjevic

Ukraine’s security service breaks up two schemes registering Starlink terminals for Russia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:34:37 UTC
23:34:37 KST

Ukraine's Security Service (SBU) said on 10 August 2026 that it had detained three more people suspected of illegally registering Starlink satellite terminals for Russian forces, and had blocked two separate schemes for putting hostile terminals onto Ukraine's verification list.
The arrests are the latest in a months-long pattern. After Ukraine and SpaceX cut off smuggled Starlink terminals and required every device to be verified, the SBU says, Russia turned to paying financially desperate Ukrainians to register hostile terminals under real Ukrainian identities, laundering them onto the whitelist meant to keep them out. Each case the service has announced follows the same script: a recruit found through Telegram, a terminal signed up in their own name, then pressure to pull in others.
In Dnipro, according to the service, counterintelligence exposed a woman who had done Russia's bidding in exchange for money to buy drugs. She registered one terminal in her own name, the SBU said, then drew in acquaintances, also looking for money for a "dose," to register more.
In Zhytomyr, according to the service, a local unemployed woman registered one terminal in her own name, then brought in a male acquaintance, and the two moved to what the SBU called "serial" activation at post offices, using front persons who handed over their personal data for a cut of the payment.
The SBU described all three suspects as drug-dependent people who had gone looking for quick money in Telegram channels, the same recruitment funnel it has flagged in earlier cases. The service said it had documented the suspects' activity, detained them at their homes, and blocked every terminal registered through the two schemes.
Investigators served the three with notices of suspicion under two articles of Ukraine's Criminal Code: Article 114-1, obstructing the lawful activity of the Armed Forces and other military formations during a special period, applied together with the prior-conspiracy provision of Article 28, and Article 111-2, aiding the aggressor state. The SBU said the suspects face eight to 12 years in prison. These are allegations, and none of the three has been convicted.
The SBU's regional directorates in Dnipropetrovsk and Zhytomyr oblasts ran the operations with the National Police, overseen by the regional prosecutors.
In February, the SBU detained a couple in Odesa Oblast who, it said, had agreed to register terminals for Russia at $30 each and planned to pull in a local resident who uses drugs to help.
In late June, the service reported eight suspected agents who registered terminals in their own names, passed the credentials to Russian handlers, and were told to recruit as many additional people as possible.
On 24 July, the SBU said it had broken up a network in Kyiv that used fake Ukrainian passports and an unwitting postal worker to activate 76 terminals for Russia's FSB security service, a case that drew high treason charges under martial law and the threat of life imprisonment.
On 25 July, the service detained a man in Dnipro suspected of registering more than 1,000 terminals, the largest single scheme reported so far.
Why Starlink matters
Starlink, operated by SpaceX, has become one of the most important communications systems on the modern battlefield, providing fast, reliable satellite internet that is difficult to jam.
Ukraine has relied extensively on the network throughout Russia's full-scale invasion to maintain communications between military units and support drone operations.
Russian forces have also sought access to the system, but their use has been significantly constrained. In cooperation with SpaceX, Ukraine has introduced verification measures and whitelisted legitimate users operating on Ukrainian territory, limiting Russian access and forcing Moscow to seek alternative communications systems.

US human rights groups sue Trump over anti-ICC push
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-11
14:34:36 UTC
23:34:36 KST

US human rights groups sue Trump over anti-ICC push
- Four US human rights groups sued the Trump administration for unconstitutional sanctions against ICC officials aiming to block justice for war crimes and crimes against humanity.
- Trump's 2025 executive order targets ICC staff for prosecuting US and Israeli officials, with the administration campaigning globally to undermine the court.
- The lawsuit follows similar legal challenges and criticism that the ICC threatens US sovereignty, with Trump stating the push defends Israel's leaders, not himself.
AI generated
WASHINGTON – Four leading US human rights groups on Aug 11 sued the Trump administration over its campaign aimed at dismantling the International Criminal Court, calling the effort unconstitutional.
US President Donald Trump in 2025 issued an executive order authorising sanctions on prosecutors, judges and others working with the court for pursuing cases against officials from the US and its ally Israel, neither of which are members of the ICC.
Human Rights Watch, the Open Society Institute, the American Friends Service Committee and the Center for Constitutional Rights said in their lawsuit filed in New York that those sanctions prevent them from working with the court to seek justice for genocide, war crimes and crimes against humanity around the world.
“The plaintiffs are seeking an end to this sanctions regime, which transgresses the President’s authority and violates international and US law, including the rights to freedom of expression and religion,” lead counsel Andrew Loewenstein of Foley Hoag, which is representing the human rights groups, said in a statement.
The lawsuit comes after a similar one filed in July by advocacy groups who work on human rights for Palestinians. In response to that lawsuit, the State Department said the groups were seeking to “enable ICC overreach” and said the court posed a threat to US sovereignty.
Three ICC judges have separately sued the Trump administration over the sanctions.
Trump’s opposition towards the ICC goes back to his first term. A similar executive order Trump issued in 2020 was blocked by a judge, who said it likely violated the First Amendment, before being rescinded by President Joe Biden’s administration in 2021.
Secretary of State Marco Rubio said in July that the administration would step up efforts to undermine the court in The Hague, including with a diplomatic campaign aimed at persuading other countries to quit it. Rubio argued that the court posed a threat to US personnel carrying out Trump’s hard-line immigration policies or strikes on boats accused of carrying drugs, as well as other US service members.
Trump, during a July 31 Cabinet meeting, said in response to an update from Rubio that the campaign was aimed at defending Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and others, rather than Trump himself. REUTERS

US human rights groups sue Trump over anti-ICC push

US human rights groups sue Trump over anti-ICC push
- Four US human rights groups sued the Trump administration for unconstitutional sanctions against ICC officials aiming to block justice for war crimes and crimes against humanity.
- Trump's 2025 executive order targets ICC staff for prosecuting US and Israeli officials, with the administration campaigning globally to undermine the court.
- The lawsuit follows similar legal challenges and criticism that the ICC threatens US sovereignty, with Trump stating the push defends Israel's leaders, not himself.
AI generated
WASHINGTON – Four leading US human rights groups on Aug 11 sued the Trump administration over its campaign aimed at dismantling the International Criminal Court, calling the effort unconstitutional.
US President Donald Trump in 2025 issued an executive order authorising sanctions on prosecutors, judges and others working with the court for pursuing cases against officials from the US and its ally Israel, neither of which are members of the ICC.
Human Rights Watch, the Open Society Institute, the American Friends Service Committee and the Center for Constitutional Rights said in their lawsuit filed in New York that those sanctions prevent them from working with the court to seek justice for genocide, war crimes and crimes against humanity around the world.
“The plaintiffs are seeking an end to this sanctions regime, which transgresses the President’s authority and violates international and US law, including the rights to freedom of expression and religion,” lead counsel Andrew Loewenstein of Foley Hoag, which is representing the human rights groups, said in a statement.
The lawsuit comes after a similar one filed in July by advocacy groups who work on human rights for Palestinians. In response to that lawsuit, the State Department said the groups were seeking to “enable ICC overreach” and said the court posed a threat to US sovereignty.
Three ICC judges have separately sued the Trump administration over the sanctions.
Trump’s opposition towards the ICC goes back to his first term. A similar executive order Trump issued in 2020 was blocked by a judge, who said it likely violated the First Amendment, before being rescinded by President Joe Biden’s administration in 2021.
Secretary of State Marco Rubio said in July that the administration would step up efforts to undermine the court in The Hague, including with a diplomatic campaign aimed at persuading other countries to quit it. Rubio argued that the court posed a threat to US personnel carrying out Trump’s hard-line immigration policies or strikes on boats accused of carrying drugs, as well as other US service members.
Trump, during a July 31 Cabinet meeting, said in response to an update from Rubio that the campaign was aimed at defending Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and others, rather than Trump himself. REUTERS
